## R-077 · ESD models, targets and dielectric breakdown

- **Industry Council targets:** ESD Association, "The Industry Council on ESD Target levels: Making an Indelible Impact on ESD from Components to Systems" (file dated June 2013): "the recommended safe levels are 1kV for HBM and 250V for CDM. These levels only require basic mandatory ESD control programs and still provide significant margin above critical levels that would warrant advanced control programs (<500V HBM, <125V CDM)"; high-speed, high-pin-count designs "are in the process of driving CDM to a lower level of 125V" [Established].
- **JEDEC publication:** ESD Association, "ESD Target Levels – the Industry Council" (undated; cites the April 2010 ESDA roadmap): "The papers were also published as JEDEC documents JEP155 (HBM) and JEP157 (CDM)"; "HBM levels between 500V to 2kV are safe for qualification … CDM levels of a minimum 250V are safe" [Established]. Texas Instruments TPS55288 datasheet (SLVSF01B, revised December 2020), ESD ratings footnote: "JEDEC document JEP155 states that 500-V HBM allows safe manufacturing with a standard ESD control process", with the matching 250 V CDM statement for JEP157 [Established].
- **Test networks and classes:** EOS/ESD Association, "Fundamentals of Electrostatic Discharge, Part Five – Device Sensitivity and Testing" (© 2024): HBM "is modeled by a 100 pF capacitor … discharged through a switching component and a 1.5 k (1,500 ohms) series resistor"; JS-001 HBM classes 0Z (< 50 V) to 3B (≥ 8000 V), with 1C = 1000 to < 2000 V; JS-002 CDM classes C0A (< 125 V) to C3 (≥ 1000 V), with C1 = 250 to < 500 V [Established]. The current HBM standard is ANSI/ESDA/JEDEC JS-001-2024 (title of the ESDA table of contents) [Established].
- **System-level network:** Murata, "Basic knowledge of ESD", chapter 2 (web page, undated): IEC 61000-4-2 uses "a 150 pF capacitor and an internal resistance of 330 Ω", tested at 2, 4, 6 and 8 kV; HBM is "Capacitor: 100 pF, internal resistance: 1.5 kΩ" [Established].
- **SiO₂ breakdown:** C. Henkel and M. Östling, KTH course IH2655 (Spring 2012), Lecture 5, "Thermal Oxidation of Silicon": "High breakdown field ( > 10^7 V/cm)"; "Dielectric constant ＝3.9" [Established].
- **Electrostatic chuck:** G. I. Shim and H. Sugai, *Plasma and Fusion Research* 3, 051 (2008), doi 10.1585/pfr.3.051: Coulomb-type and Johnsen–Rahbek-type chucks; "In the J-R type, very strong holding force is achieved at lower chuck voltages than in the Coulomb type"; for a 200 mm wafer on alumina at 1 kV, "C = ε0 εS S /l = 4.53 nF … F0 = (D²/2ε0)S = 36.9 N … agrees with the measured value F0 ∼ 40 N" [Established].
- **IEC 61000-4-2 specified waveform (contact discharge):** first-peak current 3.75 A/kV ±15% (30 A at 8 kV), 10–90% rise time 0.8 ns ±25%, and about 2 A/kV and 1 A/kV at 30 ns and 60 ns (±30%). Read from search excerpts of an ESD-generator datasheet (EM Test NX30, "30 A ± 15 % @8 kV") and an Ametek IEC 61000-4-2 workshop deck (Sep 2019); the standard itself is paywalled and esda.org could not be reached [Established].
- **Derived here:** idealized peak currents 8 kV/330 Ω = 24.24 A (IEC) and 8 kV/1.5 kΩ = 5.333 A (HBM), ignoring tester parasitics; the specified first peak (30 A at 8 kV) is higher because the generator's discharge path is not a pure RC.
- **Not verified:** the current revision dates of JEP155 (search listings show JEP155B, July 2018, reaffirmed 2024) and JEP157 (JEP157A, April 2022) come from reseller catalogs; jedec.org was unreachable. The chapter cites the documents without revision dates.
- **Sources:** [ESDA Industry Council, 2013](https://www.esda.org/assets/News/TheIndustryCouncilonESDandTheImpactonICQualificationfinalJune2013.pdf); [ESDA Industry Council summary](https://esda.org/assets/News/TheIndustryCouncilonESD.pdf); [TI TPS55288 datasheet](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/tps55288.pdf); [ESDA Fundamentals Part 5](https://esda.org/assets/Documents/Fundamentals-of-ESD-Part-5-Device-Sensitivity-and-Testing.pdf); [JS-001-2024 table of contents](https://www.esda.org/assets/Store/ToC/ANSI-ESDA-JEDEC-JS-001-2024-TOC.pdf); [Murata, Basic knowledge of ESD](https://www.murata.com/products/emc/esd/overview/basicesd/chapter2); [KTH IH2655 Lecture 5](https://www.kth.se/social/upload/51029fc0f276540764a5c298/Lecture%205.pdf); [Shim and Sugai 2008](https://www.jspf.or.jp/PFR/PDF/pfr2008_03-051.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-078 · Gauss-law applications: silicon permittivity, capacitive sensing and Coulomb's-law tests

- **Silicon relative permittivity:** C. Honsberg and S. Bowden, *PVEducation*, "General Properties of Silicon" (appendix of material properties, undated): εr = 11.7, with atomic density 5×10²² cm⁻³, band gap 1.1242 eV and intrinsic carrier concentration 1×10¹⁰ cm⁻³ at 300 K in the same table [Established].
- **Capacitive sensing:** Infineon, *AN85951 – PSOC 4 and PSOC 6 MCU CAPSENSE design guide*, document 001-85951 Rev. AJ (29 July 2026), read from the infineon.cn copy because infineon.com returned an empty body. Self-capacitance: "CP and CF are parallel to each other because both represent the capacitance between the sensor pin and ground. Therefore, the total capacitance CS of the sensor, when the finger is present on the sensor, is the sum of CP and CF", with CF = ε0εrA/d. Mutual capacitance: "When you place a finger on the CSX button, the mutual-capacitance between Rx and Tx electrodes decreases"; "The mutual capacitance between the Tx and Rx electrode should be higher than approximately 750 fF". Its Table 2 gives sensor parasitic capacitance ranges of 5–45 pF (third generation), 5–200 pF (fourth) and 2–200 pF (fifth); a typical required signal of 100 fF (third and fourth generations) and 15 fF self-capacitance / 10 fF mutual-capacitance (fifth); rms noise floors of 500 aF / 100 aF (fifth); benchmark conditions CP ≈ 33 pF and CF = 0.1 pF. Board-to-earth capacitance for mains-powered products "is usually 100 pF to 200 pF" [Established].
- **Inverse-square test:** E. R. Williams, J. E. Faller and H. A. Hill, "New Experimental Test of Coulomb's Law: A Laboratory Upper Limit on the Photon Rest Mass", *Physical Review Letters* 26(12), 721–724 (1971), doi 10.1103/PhysRevLett.26.721; abstract (via Scilit): the result "extends the validity of Coulomb's law by two orders of magnitude". The deviation of the exponent from 2, (2.7 ± 3.1)×10⁻¹⁶, is as quoted by secondary sources (a university demonstration-lab page and a 1973 *Nature* article giving "less than 3×10⁻¹⁶"); the PRL full text was not opened [Established].
- **Derived here:** a 15 fF signal over a 500 aF rms noise floor is a signal-to-noise ratio of 30.
- **Sources:** [PVEducation, General Properties of Silicon](https://www.pveducation.org/pvcdrom/materials/general-properties-of-silicon); [Infineon AN85951 Rev. AJ](https://www.infineon.cn/assets/row/public/documents/30/42/infineon-an85951-psoc-4-psoc-6-capsense-design-guide-applicationnotes-en.pdf); [Williams, Faller and Hill 1971 (Scilit record)](https://scilit.com/publications/8458729ed6cc5d9a04205904d53b5e40); [demonstration-lab page quoting the bound](https://contemporary-physicslab.github.io/Demolab/a2001-1). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-079 · Dielectric constants, capacitor materials and DRAM cells

- **High-k and low-k dielectrics:** M. Jenkins, D. Z. Austin, J. F. Conley Jr. et al. (with S. W. King, Intel), "Review—Beyond the Highs and Lows: A Perspective on the Future of Dielectrics Research for Nanoelectronic Devices", *ECS J. Solid State Sci. Technol.* 8(11), N159–N185 (21 Oct 2019), doi 10.1149/2.0161910jss, Table I: SiO₂ 3.9, HfO₂ 25, ZrO₂ 25, Al₂O₃ 9, Si₃N₄ 7 (the extracted text fuses a reference superscript onto the values). Low-k history: "SiOF … with a k of ∼ 3.7–3.9"; SiOCH at about 3.0, then "SiOCH materials with lower k values of 2.7–2.9 for 60–45 nm technologies"; porous SiOCH below 2.5; "By the mid 2010's, the challenges of implementing new low-k SiOCH ILDs with k ≤ 2.3 had reached a breaking point". The review also says the ZrO₂/Al₂O₃/ZrO₂ (ZAZ) DRAM capacitor stack "has been adopted by most DRAM manufacturers" [Established].
- **High-k range:** H. Zhu et al., *Journal of Materials Science* (2012), doi 10.1007/s10853-012-6568-y: a high-k "k value should be in the range of 10–30 (compared to 3.9 for SiO2)" [Established].
- **Roadmap:** IEEE, *International Roadmap for Devices and Systems 2022 Update: More Moore*, §4.5.3 and §5.1: "Reduction of the IMD κ value is slowing down because of problems with manufacturability"; "Difficulties associated with the integration of highly porous ultra-low-κ (κ ≤ 2) materials become clearer, and air-gap technologies is the alternative path"; DRAM capacitors use ZAZ, with perovskites (κ > 50 to 100) as a future option. The roadmap's numeric k table (MM-13) is not text in the PDF and was not quoted [Established].
- **DRAM cell capacitance:** P. J. Nair, *Architectural Techniques to Enable Reliable and Scalable Memory Systems*, Ph.D. dissertation, Georgia Tech (May 2017), arXiv:1704.03991, §3.2.1: "To meet the DRAM retention time, the capacitance stored on the DRAM device needs to be approximately 25fF." J. Choe (TechInsights), "DRAM Scaling Trend and Beyond", TechInsights blog (undated; treats D1z/D1a as current, about 2021–22): "D1z and D1a cell capacitances are now lower than 10 fF/cell"; "maintaining the cell capacitance (> 7fF/cell) becomes very difficult and will require materials with a higher dielectric constant (k > 50)" [Industry estimate].
- **MLCC DC bias:** Murata, Capacitor Guide, "The voltage characteristics of electrostatic capacitance" (28 Nov 2012): a 100 µF, 6.3 V part at 1.8 V DC — "the electrostatic capacitance of a product with X5R temperature characteristics decreases by approximately 10%, so the effective capacitance value becomes 90 uF … a Y5V characteristics product decreases by approximately 40%"; C0G, film and polymer capacitors show almost no DC-bias effect. Murata FAQ (undated): BaTiO₃ "At the Curie temperature (approx 125°C) or more, it has a cubic crystal structure" [Established].
- **EIA classes:** Murata, "Basics of capacitors [Lesson 2]" (14 Apr 2011): "EIA | COG | -55~125℃ | 0±30ppm/℃ … EIA | X5R | -55~85℃ | ±15% | X7R | -55~125℃ | ±15%"; Murata, "What is the temperature characteristics of ceramic capacitors?" (15 Oct 2012, revised 1 Dec 2023): class 1 temperature-compensating and class 2 high-dielectric-constant types under EIA-198. Knowles Capacitors, Class II dielectric sheet (undated): "Capacitors of this type have a dielectric constant range of 1000-4000" [Established]. A Murata SimSurfing document (Sept 2022) lists X5R from −25 °C, conflicting with EIA and Murata's own Lesson 2; the −55 °C value is used.
- **Not verified:** the widely cited ADI/Maxim tutorial 5527 example of a 4.7 µF 0603 X5R part losing about 93% at 5 V could not be opened (analog.com unreachable) and is not used.
- **Sources:** [Jenkins et al. 2019 (author copy)](https://web.engr.oregonstate.edu/~jconley/Jenkins%20et%20al.%20-%202019%20-%20Review%E2%80%94Beyond%20the%20Highs%20and%20Lows%20A%20Perspective%20on.pdf); [Zhu et al. 2012](https://ramprasad.mse.gatech.edu/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/75.pdf); [IRDS 2022 More Moore](https://irds.ieee.org/images/files/pdf/2022/2022IRDS_MM.pdf); [Nair 2017](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1704.03991); [TechInsights, DRAM Scaling Trend and Beyond](https://www.techinsights.com/ja/node/37840); [Murata DC-bias article](https://article.murata.com/en-global/article/voltage-characteristics-of-electrostatic-capacitance); [Murata FAQ](https://www.murata.com/support/faqs/products/capacitor/mlcc/char/0005); [Murata Lesson 2](https://article.murata.com/en-global/article/basics-of-capacitors-2); [Murata temperature characteristics](https://article.murata.com/en-global/article/temperature-characteristics-electrostatic-capacitance); [Knowles Class II dielectrics](https://www.knowlescapacitors.com/getattachment/4dc18046-0e1c-4194-a018-855f4297c79b/X7R). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-080 · Conduction: interconnect metals, electromigration and backside power

- **Bulk resistivities and mean free paths:** J.-P. Soulié, …, Z. Tőkei, C. Adelmann (imec / KU Leuven), "Selecting alternative metals for advanced interconnects", *J. Appl. Phys.* 136, 171101 (Nov 2024), read as arXiv:2406.09106v2, Table II: Cu 1.68 µΩ·cm with λ = 40.7 nm and ρ₀λ = 6.8×10⁻¹⁶ Ω·m²; Mo 5.3 µΩ·cm (ρ₀λ 5.8×10⁻¹⁶); Co 5.1 (along c) and 9.1 µΩ·cm (in plane); Ru 5.7 and 7.4 µΩ·cm. §I: "the combined thickness of barrier and liner layers cannot be reduced below 2 to 3 nm without compromising functionality"; "commercial microelectronic chips feature minimum interconnect pitches around 25 nm, with further scaling anticipated to reach sub-20 nm metal pitches". §IV: "Co has already been integrated into local interconnects of commercial CMOS technologies … Currently, barrierless Ru metallization combined with airgap structures is considered the leading candidate for logic interconnects" [Established; the roadmap pitches are Industry estimates].
- **Mean free path of copper:** D. Gall, "Electron mean free path in elemental metals", *J. Appl. Phys.* 119, 085101 (2016), abstract (via OpenAlex): "in the limit of narrow wires, Rh, Ir, and Ni are 2.1, 1.8, and 1.6 times more conductive than Cu, while various metals including Mo, Co, and Ru approximately match the Cu resistivity". T. Zhou, A. Jog and D. Gall, *Appl. Phys. Lett.* 120, 241603 (2022): "the bulk electron mean free path λ which is, for example, 39 nm for copper at room temperature"; Table 2 gives ρ₀λ = 6.70×10⁻¹⁶ Ω·m² for Cu [Established].
- **Free-electron Fermi velocity:** HyperPhysics table of Fermi energies and velocities (after Ashcroft and Mermin, *Solid State Physics*, 1976): Cu Fermi velocity 1.57×10⁶ m/s, Fermi energy 7.00 eV. Gall (2016) notes that copper's actual average Fermi velocity deviates from the free-electron value [Established].
- **Copper temperature coefficient:** Copper Development Association fact sheet and ASTM B193: 0.00393 per °C at 20 °C for annealed copper of 100% IACS conductivity, traced to IEC 60028 (whose text was not opened) [Established].
- **Silicon mobilities:** C. Hu, *Modern Semiconductor Devices for Integrated Circuits* (Pearson, 2010), Table 2-1: lightly doped Si at room temperature µₙ = 1400 and µₚ = 470 cm²/V·s. The often-quoted µₚ = 450 cm²/V·s could not be traced to an opened primary source [Established].
- **Electromigration limits:** J. Lienig and M. Thiele, "The Pressing Need for Electromigration-Aware Physical Design", *Proc. ISPD '18*, pp. 144–151 (March 2018), Table 1, reproducing ITRS 2015 for copper at 105 °C: maximum tolerable DC-equivalent current density in minimum-width M1 of 1.8, 1.1, 0.7, 0.4, 0.3 and 0.2 MA/cm² for 2018, 2020, 2022, 2024, 2026 and 2028 (values approximated from an ITRS figure), against a current density needed to drive four inverter gates rising from 2.29 to 5.35 MA/cm² [Industry estimate: a 2015 projection].
- **Molybdenum:** Lam Research press release, "Lam Research Ushers in New Era of Semiconductor Metallization with ALTUS® Halo for Molybdenum Atomic Layer Deposition" (19 Feb 2025): molybdenum "has lower resistivity in nano-scale wires than tungsten and doesn't need an adhesion or barrier layer"; "In most instances ALTUS Halo provides better than 50% improvement in resistance over conventional tungsten metallization"; Micron: the tool "has made it possible for Micron to bring molybdenum into mass production" [Established for the launch; performance is a vendor claim].
- **Backside power:** Intel, "PowerVia Test Shows Industry-Leading Performance" (5 June 2023): "standard cell utilization of more than 90% … more than 30% platform voltage droop improvement and 6% frequency benefit" (vendor claims; the release's 20A timing was superseded when Intel moved PowerVia to 18A, per TechPowerUp, 5 Sept 2024). eeNews Europe (15 Oct 2025): "Intel 18A, a process node that combines RibbonFET transistors with PowerVia backside power delivery … The first products on the node include the Intel Core Ultra series 3 processors (code-named Panther Lake)"; All About Circuits (20 Oct 2025): Panther Lake "set to ship in late 2025 with broad availability by January 2026". SemiEngineering (25 Apr 2024): TSMC's A16 with Super Power Rail "targeted for the second half of 2026"; Tom's Hardware (Apr 2024), versus N2P: "up to 10% higher clock rate … 15%–20% lower power". SemiEngineering (27 Apr 2026), "TSMC Tech Symposium 2026, By The Numbers": "A16 had been advertised earlier for production in late 2026, but Figure 1 now shows it in 2027". A search extract of TSMC's Q1 2026 call said "on track for mass production in the second half of 2026", which conflicts; tsmc.com could not be opened [Established for Intel; TSMC timing Contested].
- **Derived here:** copper's conduction-electron density 8.49×10²⁸ m⁻³ from 8.96 g/cm³, 63.546 g/mol and one electron per atom; Drude τ = 24.9 fs and λ = 39 nm from ρ and the Fermi speed; 1 oz/ft² copper = 34.06 µm.
- **Sources:** [imec tutorial, arXiv:2406.09106](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2406.09106); [Gall 2016](https://doi.org/10.1063/1.4942216); [Zhou, Jog and Gall 2022](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10349176); [HyperPhysics Fermi table](https://hyperphysics.gsu.edu/hbase/Tables/fermi.html); [Copper Development Association](https://copper.org/publications/newsletters/innovations/2001/08/intro_fac.php); [Hu, ch. 2](https://www.chu.berkeley.edu/wp-content/uploads/2020/01/Chenming-Hu_ch2-2.pdf); [Lienig and Thiele 2018](https://www.ifte.de/mitarbeiter/lienig/ispd_2018_pp144_151.pdf); [Lam Research, Feb 2025](https://newsroom.lamresearch.com/2025-02-19-Lam-Research-Ushers-in-New-Era-of-Semiconductor-Metallization-with-ALTUS-R-Halo-for-Molybdenum-Atomic-Layer-Deposition); [Intel PowerVia release, June 2023](https://d1io3yog0oux5.cloudfront.net/_984a9a34c9ce5092579cbbf000710896/intel/news/2023-06-05_PowerVia_Test_Shows_Industry_Leading_1623.pdf); [eeNews Europe, Oct 2025](https://www.eenewseurope.com/en/intel-18a-anchors-intels-panther-lake-and-xeon-6-platforms); [SemiEngineering, Apr 2024](https://semiengineering.com/tsmc-uncorks-a16-with-super-power-rail/); [SemiEngineering, Apr 2026](https://semiengineering.com/tsmc-tech-symposium-2026-by-the-numbers/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-081 · Hall sensors, magnetic core materials and MRAM

- **Hall switch:** Texas Instruments, DRV5032 datasheet, SLVSDC7H (April 2017, revised December 2024): applications include "Cell Phone, laptop, or tablet case sensing"; "Industry-leading ultra-low power consumption – 5Hz version: 0.54µA … Magnetic threshold options (maximum BOP): – DG Version: 3.4mT … – ZE Version: 63mT"; supply 1.65–5.5 V [Established].
- **Hall current sensor:** Allegro MicroSystems, ACS724 datasheet, ACS724-DS revision 25 (31 March 2026): AEC-Q100 automotive grade, SOIC-8; "High-bandwidth 120 kHz analog output"; "1.2 mΩ primary conductor resistance for low power loss"; sensitivity 40–800 mV/A by variant, "xLLCTR-05AB … Sensitivity … 400 … mV/A" (±5 A) and 40 mV/A for ±50 A at VCC = 5 V; "Dielectric Strength Test Voltage … VISO … 2400 VRMS" [Established].
- **ACS724 stray-field rejection:** datasheet feature list (search excerpts of the SOIC8 datasheet and distributor copies): "Differential Hall sensing rejects common-mode fields"; electrical characteristics list a common-mode field rejection ratio of 40 dB (typical) for a uniform external field. Allegro application note AN269123 attributes the ~40 dB to ~1% Hall-plate matching. The official PDF was not reopened [Established].
- **Ferrite:** TDK Electronics, "SIFERRIT material N87" datasheet (June 2025): "Flux density BS (25 °C) mT 490 (H = 1200 A/m, f = 10 kHz) BS (100 °C) mT 390"; optimum frequency range 25–500 kHz [Established].
- **Powder and steel cores:** Magnetics Inc. product pages (accessed 18 Nov 2026): High Flux (50Ni-50Fe) cores have "saturation flux density that is twice that of MPP and three times or more that of ferrite … (15,000 gauss)"; Kool Mµ (FeSiAl) has a "relatively high saturation level (10,500 gauss)"; Magnesil, "a grain-oriented 3% silicon-iron alloy … Flux Density (T): 1.50 to 1.80" [Established].
- **Embedded MRAM:** Samsung Newsroom (6 Mar 2019): "commenced mass production of its first commercial embedded magnetic random access memory (eMRAM) product based on the company's 28-nanometer(nm) fully-depleted silicon-on-insulator (FD-SOI) process technology, called 28FDS". GlobalFoundries press release (27 Feb 2020; read via the Design & Reuse reprint because the gf.com page returns 404): eMRAM "on the company's 22nm FD-SOI (22FDX®) platform has entered production … demonstrated 100,000-cycle endurance and 10-year data retention across the -40°C to 125°C temperature range". NXP press release (16 May 2023; read via the EEJournal reprint): "TSMC's 16FinFET embedded MRAM technology exceeds … one-million cycle endurance, support for solder reflow, and 20-year data retention at 150°C", with first S32 products to sample in early 2025 [Established for the announcements; performance figures are vendor claims].
- **Sources:** [TI DRV5032](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/drv5032.pdf); [Allegro ACS724](https://www.allegromicro.com/-/media/files/datasheets/acs724-datasheet.pdf); [TDK N87](https://www.tdk-electronics.tdk.com/download/528882/d6940b239127a8bab71b22168746021b/pdf-n87.pdf); [Magnetics High Flux](https://www.mag-inc.com/products/powder-cores/high-flux-cores); [Magnetics Kool Mµ](https://www.mag-inc.com/products/powder-cores/kool-mu-cores); [Magnetics Magnesil](https://www.mag-inc.com/products/tape-wound-cores/magnesil); [Samsung eMRAM](https://news.samsung.com/global/samsung-electronics-starts-commercial-shipment-of-emram-product-based-on-28nm-fd-soi-process); [GlobalFoundries eMRAM (reprint)](https://www.design-reuse.com/news/47614/globalfoundries-demram-22fdx-iot-automotive.html); [NXP and TSMC eMRAM (reprint)](https://www.eejournal.com/industry_news/nxp-and-tsmc-to-deliver-industrys-first-automotive-16-nm-finfet-embedded-mram/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-082 · Inductive power transfer, power inductors and integrated magnetics

- **Qi history:** Wireless Power Consortium, "History of the Qi Specifications" (Knowledge Base, accessed 18 Nov 2026): "Version 1.2 (October 2015) … up to 15 watt"; "Version 2.0 (April 2023) Introduction of the Magnetic Power Profile (MPP) Magnetic attachment and alignment … 360 kHz operating frequency"; "Version 2.2 (April 2025) Support for up to 25 watt power transfer"; "The current version of the Qi specification is version number 2.2.1." Version 1.0 (2010) supported 5 W. Version 2.0 is "not required to be 100% backwards compatible with Qi 1.x Certified Products" [Established].
- **Qi2 25W:** WPC news release, "WPC Ushers in Next Generation of Faster Wireless Charging" (Washington D.C., 23 July 2025): launch of "Qi v2.2.1 – which will be branded as Qi2 25W … Qi2 … was launched in November 2023 with 15 watts of charging power"; more than 1.5 billion Qi2-certified devices within a year of introduction [Established; the device count is a WPC self-reported Industry estimate].
- **Power inductor:** Coilcraft Document 856-1, "Shielded Power Inductors – XAL7070" (revised 25 July 2012; read from a Farnell-hosted copy because coilcraft.com was blocked): "XAL7070-102ME_ 1.0 2.55 2.81 64 34.8 20.0 25.0" (L = 1.0 µH, DCR 2.55 mΩ typical and 2.81 mΩ maximum, SRF 64 MHz, Isat 34.8 A, Irms 20.0 A for a 20 °C rise); "DC current at which the inductance drops 30% (typ) from its value without current"; marketed for VRM/VRD applications. A search extract of Coilcraft's current product page shows DCR 2.6 typical / 2.8 maximum, apparently rounding [Established].
- **Integrated voltage regulators:** E. A. Burton et al. (Intel), "FIVR — Fully Integrated Voltage Regulators on 4th Generation Intel® Core™ SoCs", *IEEE APEC 2014*, pp. 432–439, doi 10.1109/APEC.2014.6803344 (full text from a third-party mirror; abstract confirmed via OpenAlex): "These 140 MHz multi-phase buck regulators are integrated into the 22nm processor die, and feature up to 80 MHz unity gain bandwidth, non-magnetic package trace inductors and on-die MIM capacitors"; "this allowed 59 inductors on 10 different voltage rails to be implemented in a 20mm x 8mm area"; about 90% efficiency at 1.7 V to 1.05 V [Established].
- **On-chip inductor Q:** C. P. Yue and S. S. Wong, "On-chip spiral inductors with patterned ground shields for Si-based RF ICs", *IEEE JSSC* 33(5):743–752 (May 1998), abstract via OpenAlex: "At 1-2 GHz, the addition of the shield increases the inductor quality factor up to 33% … the quality factor of a 2-GHz LC tank can be nearly doubled with a shielded inductor". C.-L. Dai, J.-Y. Hong and M.-C. Liu, "High Q-factor CMOS-MEMS inductor", DTIP 2008 (arXiv:0805.0915): a spiral with the substrate removed reached "a Q-factor of 15 at 11 GHz", and the paper reviews CMOS-compatible inductors with maximum Q of about 4.7 to 15. A. M. Niknejad, UC Berkeley EE242B lecture (12 Sept 2024), shows measured 90 nm CMOS resonators with Q = 8.8 and 15. Summary used: Q ≈ 5–20 at 1–20 GHz in standard CMOS [Established as an order of magnitude; no foundry-PDK typical value was found].
- **Derived here:** for the illustrative Example 04-07.8, the ripple $(V_{in} - V_{out})D/(fL)$ with an assumed 2 nH inductor at 140 MHz; the inductance value is not from the FIVR paper.
- **Sources:** [WPC Qi history](https://www.wirelesspowerconsortium.com/knowledge-base/qi-specification/history-of-the-qi-specifications/); [WPC Qi2 25W release](https://www.wirelesspowerconsortium.com/media/wvsmia32/qi2-25w-news-release-v72.pdf); [Coilcraft XAL7070](https://www.farnell.com/datasheets/1681997.pdf); [Burton et al. 2014](https://xdevs.com/doc/_Metrology/burton2014.pdf); [Yue and Wong 1998](https://doi.org/10.1109/4.668989); [Dai et al. 2008](https://arxiv.org/pdf/0805.0915); [Niknejad EE242B](https://neu-ucb-tvip.github.io/tvip-analog-workspace/resources/Niknejad_242B_Module_1_3_Passives-2.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-083 · Board dielectrics, propagation, 5G bands and RF exposure

- **FR-4-class laminate:** Isola 370HR datasheet, Rev. C (last updated 15 Apr 2021): "Dk, Permittivity … @ 100 MHz 4.24 … @ 1 GHz 4.17 … @ 2 GHz 4.04 … @ 10 GHz 3.92" and "Df, Loss Tangent … @ 1 GHz 0.0161 … @ 10 GHz 0.0250"; table values used: Dk 4.24/4.17/4.04/3.92/3.92 and Df 0.0150/0.0161/0.0210/0.0250/0.0250 at 0.1/1/2/5/10 GHz (typical; resin content not stated in the extracted text) [Established].
- **Low-loss laminate:** Rogers Corporation, "RO4000® Series High Frequency Circuit Materials" datasheet (© 2022, revised 080322, PUB# 92-004): RO4350B "Dielectric Constant, er Process … 3.48 ± 0.05 … 10 GHz/23°C"; "Dielectric Constant, er Design … 3.66 … 8 to 40 GHz"; "Dissipation Factor … 0.0037 … 10 GHz/23°C" and 0.0031 at 2.5 GHz [Established].
- **Propagation on boards:** Texas Instruments, "High-Speed Layout Guidelines", Application Report SCAA082A (Nov 2006, revised Aug 2017), §1.3.1 and Table 2: FR-4 with εr = 4.6, 100 mm trace, 1.5 mm height; "Stripline 1 0.5 4.6 0.466 139.8 715.3" (width mm, εr,eff, vp/c, vp mm/ns, delay ps per 100 mm); microstrip with εr,eff ≈ 3.0–3.2 gives 581.7–593.1 ps per 100 mm [Established].
- **5G NR frequency ranges:** ETSI TS 138 104 V19.6.0 (2026-09), equal to 3GPP TS 38.104 version 19.6.0 Release 19, Table 5.1-1: "FR1 410 MHz – 7125 MHz … FR2-1 24250 MHz – 52600 MHz … FR2-2 52600 MHz – 71000 MHz"; Table 5.2-2 TDD bands include n257 26 500–29 500 MHz, n258 24 250–27 500 MHz, n259 39 500–43 500 MHz, n260 37 000–40 000 MHz, n261 27 500–28 350 MHz, n262 47 200–48 200 MHz and n263 57 000–71 000 MHz [Established].
- **US exposure limits:** 47 CFR §1.1310 (as amended at 85 FR 18145, 1 Apr 2020; eCFR read 18 Nov 2026): "The SAR limits for general population/uncontrolled exposure are 0.08 W/kg, as averaged over the whole body, and a peak spatial-average SAR of 1.6 W/kg, averaged over any 1 gram of tissue"; extremities 4 W/kg over 10 g; SAR applies from 100 kHz to 6 GHz; general-population MPE of 1.0 mW/cm² from 1.5 to 100 GHz, averaged over 30 minutes [Established].
- **ICNIRP 2020:** "Guidelines for Limiting Exposure to Electromagnetic Fields (100 kHz to 300 GHz)", *Health Physics* 118(5):483–524 (2020), doi 10.1097/HP.0000000000001210, Table 2: general public "100 kHz to 6 GHz 0.08 2 4 NA … >6 to 300 GHz 0.08 NA NA 20" (whole-body W/kg; local head/torso W/kg; limbs W/kg; absorbed power density W/m² over 4 cm²); "Local SAR is to be averaged over a 10-g cubic mass" [Established].
- **Derived here:** stripline delay 7.154 ps/mm from √4.6/c (TI's 715.3 ps per 100 mm uses a rounded speed); dielectric attenuation πf√εr tan δ/c; the averaging-mass comparison of Problem 04-08.13 is illustrative.
- **Not verified:** Panasonic Megtron 6 Dk/Df (the manufacturer's site was unreachable) is not used.
- **Sources:** [Isola 370HR](https://www.isola-group.com/wp-content/uploads/data-sheets/370hr.pdf); [Rogers RO4000](https://www.rogerscorp.com/-/media/project/rogerscorp/documents/advanced-electronics-solutions/english/data-sheets/ro4000-laminates-ro4003c-and-ro4350b---data-sheet.pdf); [TI SCAA082A](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/scaa082a/scaa082a.pdf); [ETSI TS 138 104 V19.6.0](https://www.etsi.org/deliver/etsi_ts/138100_138199/138104/19.06.00_60/ts_138104v190600p.pdf); [47 CFR §1.1310](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-A/part-1/subpart-I/section-1.1310); [ICNIRP 2020](https://www.icnirp.org/cms/upload/publications/ICNIRPrfgdl2020.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-084 · Optical interconnect standards, fiber, EUV and co-packaged optics

- **400GBASE-DR4:** IEEE P802.3bs Task Force, *IEEE P802.3bs Baseline Summary* (J. D'Ambrosia, 18 Jul 2015), recording the adopted "400G-PSM4" baseline: "Move to adopt a baseline for the 500m SMF proposal based on welch_3bs_01a_0715 as 400GBASE-DR4"; "Reach, min (m) 500 … Encoding type PAM4 … Wavelength(s) 1304.5 to 1317.5 nm". IEEE Std 802.3bs-2017 approved 6 Dec 2017 [Established; final Clause 124 not opened].
- **400GBASE-FR4:** D. Lewis (Lumentum), "400GBASE-FR4 Baseline Proposal", lewis_3cu_01a_0519 (IEEE 802.3cu, 23 May 2019), adopted by Motion #7: lanes "L0 1271 1264.5 to 1277.5 / L1 1291 1284.5 to 1297.5 / L2 1311 1304.5 to 1317.5 / L3 1331 1324.5 to 1337.5" nm for "operation over SMF with lengths up to at least 2 km". IEEE Std 802.3cu-2021 approved 9 Feb 2021 [Established; final Clause 151 not opened].
- **400GBASE-SR8:** J. King, king_3cm_01b_0518 (IEEE P802.3cm, May 2018), adopted as Motion #4: "8 parallel lanes @ 26.5625 GBd for 400GBASE-SR8 over 100 m OM4 fiber"; "850 nm sources". IEEE Std 802.3cm-2020 approved 30 Jan 2020 [Established].
- **800G and 1.6T objectives:** *Adopted IEEE P802.3df Objectives* (17 Nov 2022): "over 8 pairs of SMF with lengths up to at least 500 m • over 8 pairs of SMF with lengths up to at least 2 km"; IEEE Std 802.3df-2024 approved 16 Feb 2024. *Adopted IEEE P802.3dj Objectives* (14 Mar 2024), with 200 Gb/s lanes: "over 4 wavelengths over a single SMF in each direction with lengths up to at least 2 km" for 800 Gb/s, and 1.6 Tb/s over 8 SMF pairs to 500 m [Established for objectives; P802.3dj final approval not verified].
- **Fiber:** Recommendation ITU-T G.652 (08/2024), Table 2 (G.652.D): "Attenuation coefficient Maximum from 1310 nm to 1625 nm 0.40 dB/km … Maximum at 1530-1565 nm 0.30 dB/km"; Table I.1 (link-planning values): "1260 nm-1360 nm 0.5 dB/km 1530 nm-1565 nm 0.275 dB/km … D1550 17 ps/(nm × km)" [Established; these are specification and planning values, not bare-fiber typicals].
- **EUV and High-NA:** C. Middleton, "5 things you should know about High NA EUV lithography", ASML Stories (25 Jan 2024): "It uses 13.5 nm light, compared to 193 nm for the highest-resolution DUV systems"; "we've increased the NA from 0.33 in our NXE systems to 0.55 in EXE systems"; "The new platform, known as EXE, offers chipmakers a CD of 8 nm. That means they can print transistors 1.7 times smaller – and therefore achieve transistor densities 2.9 times higher"; the NXE platform (first shipped 2010) brought CD to 13 nm; "After a decade of R&D, ASML shipped the first modules of the first High NA EUV lithography system to Intel in December 2023". ASML, "The Rayleigh criterion for resolution" (undated): "CD = k1 • λ / NA … The physical limit lithography is k1 = 0.25". ASML product pages (read Nov 2026): NXE:3800E 0.33 NA, 13 nm, ≥ 220 wafers/h at 30 mJ/cm²; EXE:5000 0.55 NA, 8 nm, ≥ 110 wafers/h at 50 mJ/cm², exposure field half the size of NXE; EXE:5200B 0.55 NA, 8 nm, ≥ 175 wafers/h at 50 mJ/cm² [Established for wavelength, NA and specifications; Industry estimate for the 1.7×/2.9× framing].
- **Co-packaged optics (vendor claims):** NVIDIA press release (GTC, 18 Mar 2025): Spectrum-X and Quantum-X Photonics switches with "4x fewer lasers to deliver 3.5x more power efficiency, 63x greater signal integrity, 10x better network resiliency at scale and 1.3x faster deployment compared with traditional methods"; Quantum-X "expected to be available later this year, with NVIDIA Spectrum-X Photonics Ethernet switches coming in 2026". NVIDIA Technical Blog (A. Seyedi, 18 Aug 2025): pluggables' "higher power draw (often 30W per interface)" against CPO "as low as 9W"; "Commercial availability is planned for early 2026 for Quantum-X InfiniBand switches and the second half of 2026 for Spectrum-X Ethernet switches"; the blog's figure caption says "64x better signal integrity". Broadcom press release (8 Oct 2025; read from distributed copies because investors.broadcom.com returned 403): Tomahawk 6 – Davisson, 102.4 Tb/s, "16 x 6.4 Tbps Davisson DR Optical Engines"; "The result is a 70% reduction in optical interconnect power consumption—more than 3.5x lower than traditional pluggable solutions"; "now shipping" and "currently sampling the TH6-Davisson BCM78919 device to its early access customers and partners" [Industry estimate; shipments not confirmed by a primary source].
- **Derived here:** implied k₁ = 13 × 0.33/13.5 = 0.318 (NXE) and 8 × 0.55/13.5 = 0.326 (EXE); a 70% cut is a 3.33× reduction, while "more than 3.5×" implies at least 71.4%; 13.5 nm photons carry hc/λ ≈ 91.8 eV.
- **Sources:** [802.3bs baseline](https://www.ieee802.org/3/bs/baseline_3bs_0715.pdf); [802.3cu FR4 baseline](https://www.ieee802.org/3/cu/public/May19/lewis_3cu_01a_0519.pdf); [802.3cm SR8 baseline](https://www.ieee802.org/3/cm/public/May18/king_3cm_01b_0518.pdf); [802.3df objectives](https://www.ieee802.org/3/df/proj_doc/objectives_P802d3df_221117.pdf); [802.3dj objectives](https://www.ieee802.org/3/dj/projdoc/objectives_P802d3dj_240314.pdf); [ITU-T G.652 (2024)](https://www.itu.int/rec/T-REC-G.652-202408-I/en); [ASML High-NA story](https://www.asml.com/en/news/stories/2024/5-things-high-na-euv); [ASML Rayleigh criterion](https://www.asml.com/en/technology/lithography-principles/rayleigh-criterion); [ASML EXE:5200B](https://www.asml.com/en/products/euv-lithography-systems/twinscan-exe-5200b); [NVIDIA press release](https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-spectrum-x-co-packaged-optics-networking-switches-ai-factories); [NVIDIA blog](https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/scaling-ai-factories-with-co-packaged-optics-for-better-power-efficiency/); [Broadcom release (copy)](https://mfn.se/one/a/broadcom/broadcom-announces-tomahawk-r-6-davisson-the-industrys-first-102-4-tbps-ethernet-switch-with-co-packaged-optics-824cfbb5). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-085 · Quantum effects in devices and quantum computing

- **Gate-oxide tunneling and high-k (Intel 45 nm):** Intel Corp. press release (Business Wire, 27 Jan 2007), "Intel's Transistor Technology Breakthrough Represents Biggest Change to Computer Chips in 40 Years": "Intel has successfully shrunk the silicon dioxide gate dielectric to as little as 1.2nm thick -- equal to five atomic layers -- on our previous 65nm process technology"; "Intel replaced the silicon dioxide with a thicker hafnium-based high-k material in the gate dielectric, reducing leakage by more than 10 times compared to the silicon dioxide used for more than four decades." M. T. Bohr, R. S. Chau, T. Ghani and K. Mistry, "The High-k Solution", *IEEE Spectrum* (1 Oct 2007): "This leakage arises from quantum effects. At 1.2 nm, the quantum nature of particles starts to play a big role." [Established]. The IEDM 2007 per-device leakage split could not be verified from a primary source and is not used.
- **Flash and Fowler–Nordheim tunneling:** P. Pavan, R. Bez, P. Olivo and E. Zanoni, "Flash Memory Cells—An Overview", *Proc. IEEE* 85(8), 1248–1271 (Aug 1997), DOI 10.1109/5.622505: "An optimum thickness (about 10 nm) is chosen in present devices"; "A direct tunneling mechanism fixes the tunnel oxide limit to 6 nm, which needs to be increased more realistically up to 7–8 nm due to trap-assisted electron tunneling caused by oxide aging"; p. 1263: "at least 8–9 MV/cm for FN data alteration in 0.1–1 s" and, comparing 7 and 10 MV/cm, "There is a variation of about seven orders of magnitude in tunnel current." [Established physics; thicknesses are 1997-era values].
- **Photon energy and silicon's absorption edge:** C. Hu, *Modern Semiconductor Devices for Integrated Circuits* (Pearson, 2010; chapters posted by the author), ch. 1: "The minimum photon energy required to induce photoconductivity is 1.1 eV"; ch. 4, eq. (4.12.2): photon energy (eV) = 1.24/λ (µm). C. Honsberg and S. Bowden, *PVEducation*, "Energy of Photon": "The exact value of 1 × 10^6 (hc/q) is 1.2398 but the approximation 1.24 is sufficient for most purposes." [Established].
- **Google Willow (surface code below threshold):** H. Neven, "Meet Willow, our state-of-the-art quantum chip", Google blog (9 Dec 2024): "With 105 qubits…"; Google Quantum AI (R. Acharya et al.), "Quantum error correction below the surface code threshold", arXiv:2408.13687, *Nature* 638, 920–926 (2025): logical error "suppressed by a factor of Λ = 2.14 ± 0.02 when increasing the code distance by two, culminating in a 101-qubit distance-7 code with 0.143% ± 0.003% error per cycle of error correction." [Established for the QEC result; Contested for the blog's 10²⁵-year random-circuit-sampling comparison].
- **IBM roadmap:** IBM press release (12 Nov 2025): Nighthawk with "120 qubits linked together with 218 next-generation tunable couplers to their four nearest neighbors in a square lattice"; targets "quantum advantage by the end of 2026 and fault-tolerant quantum computing by 2029". IBM press release (11 Nov 2026): "Introduced in September, IBM Quantum Nighthawk r2 delivers up to 100,000 samples per second while supporting accurate computations with more than 7,500 quantum operations on 120 qubits"; r3 "designed to support 10,000 quantum operations on 520 programmable qubits, with 2,034 total physical qubits", with two r3 chips to be linked into a system of more than 1,000 qubits in 2027 [Industry estimate for roadmap targets; Contested for "quantum advantage" claims, which IBM posts to a community tracker for verification].
- **Quantinuum Helios:** Quantinuum blog, "Introducing Helios: The Most Accurate Quantum Computer in the World" (5 Nov 2025): "98 fully connected physical qubits with single-qubit gate fidelity of 99.9975% and two-qubit gate fidelity of 99.921% across all qubit pairs"; "we switched our qubits from ytterbium to barium"; "94 logical qubits, fully entangled in one of the largest GHZ states ever recorded"; and, in the same post, "48 fully error-corrected logical qubits at a remarkable 2:1 encoding rate" [Industry estimate for fidelities; Contested for logical-qubit counts, which depend on the code and on detection versus correction].
- **Constants:** NIST CODATA (2022 set, read Nov 2026): h = 6.626 070 15 × 10⁻³⁴ J Hz⁻¹, e = 1.602 176 634 × 10⁻¹⁹ C, c = 299 792 458 m/s and k = 1.380 649 × 10⁻²³ J/K, all exact under the 2019 SI [Established].
- **Derived here (Fowler–Nordheim):** the textbook FN law $J\propto F^2e^{-B/F}$ with a 3.1 eV barrier and 0.5 $m_0$ gives $B$ ≈ 264 MV/cm and a current ratio of about $1.6\times10^5$ between 7 and 10 MV/cm; the 04-10 lab's transfer-matrix model gives $2.2\times10^4$. Pavan et al.'s "about seven orders of magnitude" refers to real cells; the chapter presents both.
- **Derived here:** hc/e = 1239.84 eV·nm; silicon's edge 1239.84/1.12 ≈ 1107 nm; photon energies 1.459 eV (850 nm), 0.946 eV (1310 nm) and 0.800 eV (1550 nm), the last two below silicon's gap; hypothetical Willow extrapolation 0.143%/2.14³ ≈ 0.0146% at distance 13 (an illustration, not a reported result).
- **Sources:** [Intel release](https://www.intc.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/1065/intels-transistor-technology-breakthrough-represents); [IEEE Spectrum](https://spectrum.ieee.org/the-highk-solution); [Pavan et al. (copy)](https://people.engr.tamu.edu/ajiang/FlashMemoryCells.pdf); [Hu ch. 1–3](https://www.chu.berkeley.edu/wp-content/uploads/2020/01/Chenming-Hu_ch1-3.pdf); [Hu ch. 4](https://www.chu.berkeley.edu/wp-content/uploads/2020/01/Chenming-Hu_ch4-1.pdf); [PVEducation](https://www.pveducation.org/pvcdrom/properties-of-sunlight/energy-of-photon); [Google blog](https://blog.google/technology/research/google-willow-quantum-chip/); [arXiv:2408.13687](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.13687); [IBM 2025](https://newsroom.ibm.com/2025-11-12-ibm-delivers-new-quantum-processors,-software,-and-algorithm-breakthroughs-on-path-to-advantage-and-fault-tolerance); [IBM 2026](https://newsroom.ibm.com/2026-11-11-ibm-quantum-nighthawk-demonstrates-new-levels-of-speed-and-quality,-with-plans-to-scale-beyond-1,000-qubits-in-2027); [Quantinuum Helios](https://www.quantinuum.com/blog/introducing-helios-the-most-accurate-quantum-computer-in-the-world); [NIST h](https://physics.nist.gov/cgi-bin/cuu/Value?h). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-086 · Crystals, band gaps, intrinsic carriers and wide-bandgap markets

- **Silicon lattice parameter:** NIST CODATA 2022, "lattice parameter of silicon": "Numerical value 5.431 020 511 x 10^-10 m Standard uncertainty 0.000 000 089 x 10^-10 m" (defined for an ideal crystal at reference conditions not shown on the page) [Established]. With 8 atoms per diamond cubic cell this gives 4.994×10²² atoms/cm³; PVEducation's "General Properties of Silicon" lists "Atomic Density 5 x 10^22 cm^-3 … Energy Bandgap (EG) 1.1242 eV … Lattice Constant 0.543095 nm" and effective densities of states Nc = 3×10¹⁹ and Nv = 1×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ [Established; rounded].
- **Band gaps:** C. Hu, *Modern Semiconductor Devices for Integrated Circuits* (Pearson, 2010), Table 1–1: "InSb Ge Si GaAs GaP ZnSe Diamond Eg (eV) 0.18 0.67 1.12 1.42 2.25 2.7 6.0". J. Y. Tsao et al., "Ultrawide-Bandgap Semiconductors: Research Opportunities and Challenges", *Adv. Electron. Mater.* 4, 1600501 (2018), Table 1: "GaN 4H-SiC AlGaN/AlN β-Ga2O3 Diamond Bandgap (eV) 3.4 3.3 Up to 6.0 4.9 5.5" [Established at two significant figures]. Diamond is 6.0 eV in Hu and 5.5 eV in Tsao et al.; the chapter uses 5.5 and flags the difference. Germanium is quoted as 0.66–0.67 eV (Hu gives 0.67; the 0.66 eV value common in other tables was not verified from a primary source here).
- **4H-SiC gap spread:** J. Burin, P. Gaggl, S. Waid, A. Gsponer and T. Bergauer, "TCAD Parameters for 4H-SiC: A Review", arXiv:2410.06798v8 (19 Dec 2025), §VI: "At room temperature the most commonly used values for Eg include 3.2 eV, 3.23 eV and 3.26 eV. We already showed direct connections between these values and the exciton band gap at low temperatures" [Contested at the second decimal].
- **Silicon n_i:** A. B. Sproul and M. A. Green, *J. Appl. Phys.* 70, 846 (1991): "the commonly cited value of 1.45 x 10(10) cm-3 for the silicon intrinsic carrier concentration at 300 K is inconsistent with the best experimental data. An alternate value of 1.08 x 10(10) cm-3 was proposed" (abstract read on a mirror). P. P. Altermatt, A. Schenk, F. Geelhaar and G. Heiser, *J. Appl. Phys.* 93, 1598 (2003): "The commonly used value … is ni = 1.00×10^10 cm–3" … "thereby obtaining ni = 9.65×10^9 cm–3 at 300 K" (abstract read on the UNSW repository). PVEducation, "Intrinsic Carrier Concentration": fit n_i(T) = 5.29×10¹⁹ (T/300)^2.54 exp(−6726/T) cm⁻³ (Misiakos and Tsamakis), which gives 9.70×10⁹ cm⁻³ at 300 K [Established].
- **SiO₂ band gap ≈ 9 eV:** search-result excerpts only: a study of ultrathin gate oxides cites "SiO2 band gap of 8.90 eV as recently obtained for ultrathin gate oxides by the analysis of the O 1s plasmon loss peak", and oxides grown with nitric-acid vapor gave 8.39 eV. The papers themselves (diposit.ub.edu, arxiv.org) could not be opened from the build environment [Established as "about 9 eV"; exact value depends on growth; see Open issue 17].
- **Silicon molar mass 28.0855 g/mol:** IUPAC/CIAAW standard atomic weight 28.0855(3) before 2009, since replaced by an interval for silicon; seen in search excerpts only because ciaaw.org could not be reached [Established; see Open issue 17].
- **Power SiC market:** Yole Group, *Power SiC 2026*, as reported in "Power SiC enters the AI age, says Yole", *Compound Semiconductor* (16 Jun 2026): "Yole Group forecasts a power SiC device market of $11 billion by 2031, growing at a 20 percent CAGR between 2025-2031"; "By 2031, 800V platforms are expected to represent approximately half of global BEV shipments" [Industry estimate].
- **xEV power devices:** Yole Group, *Automotive Powertrain and Electrification 2026*, as reported in "xEV power chip market to more than double to $14.5bn by 2031", *Semiconductor Today* (17 Aug 2026): "silicon carbide (SiC) MOSFETs are rapidly gaining share, growing at a 21.5% CAGR during 2025–2031, driven largely by China's BEV fleet"; Si IGBT modules "almost $7bn"; "Bosch, Renesas and Rohm are all reassessing their SiC manufacturing footprint amid overcapacity" [Industry estimate].
- **Power GaN market:** Yole Group, *Power GaN 2026*, as reported by *Semiconductor Today* (20 Aug 2026): $3.5 billion in 2031 at a 35% CAGR; "Consumer and mobile electronics remain the market's anchor segment by revenue, expected to reach $1.7bn in 2031"; "Innoscience held the top spot in 2025". The 2025 edition had forecast "$3bn by 2030 at 42% CAGR" [Industry estimate]. yolegroup.com served a bot challenge, so trade-press reprints were used.
- **Derived here:** copper free-electron values from n = 8.49×10²⁸ m⁻³ (E_F 7.044 eV, v_F 1.574×10⁶ m/s, T_F 8.17×10⁴ K); silicon n_i ≈ 6.8×10⁹ cm⁻³ from the rounded Nc, Nv; implied 2025 market sizes $3.68 billion (SiC) and $0.58 billion (GaN) by discounting the 2031 forecasts at their CAGRs.
- **Sources:** [NIST silicon lattice parameter](https://physics.nist.gov/cgi-bin/cuu/Value?asil); [Hu ch. 1–3](https://www.chu.berkeley.edu/wp-content/uploads/2020/01/Chenming-Hu_ch1-3.pdf); [Tsao et al. (copy)](https://djena.engineering.cornell.edu/papers-new/2017/tsao2018ultrawide.pdf); [arXiv:2410.06798](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.06798); [PVEducation silicon](https://www.pveducation.org/pvcdrom/materials/general-properties-of-silicon); [PVEducation n_i](https://www.pveducation.org/pvcdrom/pn-junctions/intrinsic-carrier-concentration); [Altermatt et al.](https://unsworks.unsw.edu.au/entities/publication/2c330f15-ece8-4dfd-b035-ec5d867c1d8d/full); [Compound Semiconductor](https://compoundsemiconductor.net/article/124477/Power_SiC_enters_the_AI_age_says_Yole); [Semiconductor Today, xEV](https://semiconductor-today.com/news_items/2026/aug/yole-170826.shtml); [Semiconductor Today, GaN](https://semiconductor-today.com/news_items/2026/aug/yole-200826.shtml). ## R-087 · Python releases, floating point and developer usage

- **Python releases:** python.org Downloads page (read Nov 2026): "Python 3.15.2 Oct. 25, 2026 … Python 3.15.1 Oct. 21, 2026 … Python 3.15.0 Oct. 9, 2026"; Python Developer's Guide, "Status of Python versions": 3.15 bugfix, first released 2026-10-09, end of life 2031-10; 3.10 reached end of life on 2026-10-01; "The main branch is currently the future Python 3.16". PEP 790 lists 3.15.2 on 2026-10-26 (a time-zone difference with python.org) [Established].
- **Release cadence:** Ł. Langa, PEP 602, "Annual Release Cycle for Python" (Active): "feature versions are released predictably every twelve months, in October every year"; two years of bugfix releases, then three years of security fixes (two full years from 3.13 on). PEP 2026, "Calendar versioning for Python": "Status : Rejected" [Established].
- **Free-threaded CPython:** PEP 703 (Final, Python 3.13) and PEP 779 (Final, resolution 16 Jun 2025): "Phase II would make the free-threaded build officially supported but still optional"; "What's new in Python 3.14": "The free-threaded build of Python is now supported and no longer experimental … The performance penalty on single-threaded code in free-threaded mode is now roughly 5-10%, depending on the platform and C compiler used." No decision to make it the default was found as of Nov 2026 [Established for status; Industry estimate for the penalty].
- **NumPy 2.0:** NumPy news (16 Jun 2024): "NumPy 2.0.0 is the first major release since 2006 … It includes breaking changes that could not happen in a regular minor release - including an ABI break, changes to type promotion rules, and API changes"; release notes: type promotion now follows NEP 50, which "can lead to changes in output dtypes and lower precision results for mixed-[dtype operations]". NumPy 2.5.0 was released 21 Jun 2026 [Established].
- **Floating point:** IEEE SA, "IEEE 754-2019 — IEEE Standard for Floating-Point Arithmetic": "Active Standard … Superseding 754-2008 … Published: 2019-07-22"; a revision project, P754, is open. Python Tutorial §15: "almost all platforms map Python floats to IEEE 754 binary64 'double precision' values. IEEE 754 binary64 values contain 53 bits of precision"; the stored 0.1 is "3602879701896397 / 2 ** 55". Checked locally: `sys.float_info.epsilon == 2**-52` [Established].
- **Reproducibility details:** "What's New in Python 3.12": `sum()` now uses Neumaier compensated summation for floats (gh-100425), so float sums can differ in the last bits from 3.11 (a CPython issue, gh-111933, reports a sum that was exactly 0 on 3.11 and is not on 3.12). NumPy, NEP 19 "Random number generator policy" (Final) and the `numpy.random` compatibility policy: the legacy `RandomState` stream is frozen, while `Generator` carries no promise of identical streams across NumPy releases [Established].
- **Usage surveys:** Stack Overflow Developer Survey 2026 (30,903 valid responses; language question n = 14,099): "Python 8,184 58.0% … C++ 3,425 24.3% C 3,070 21.8% Rust 2,542 18.0%". Survey 2025 (49,009 responses): Python 57.9%, C 22%, C++ 23.5%; "Python's adoption … saw a 7 percentage point increase from 2024 to 2025"; "Rust is yet again the most admired programming language (72%)". Self-selected samples recruited through Stack Overflow, which over-represent web and back-end developers [Industry estimate].
- **Sources:** [python.org downloads](https://www.python.org/downloads/); [devguide versions](https://devguide.python.org/versions/); [PEP 602](https://peps.python.org/pep-0602/); [PEP 2026](https://peps.python.org/pep-2026/); [PEP 779](https://peps.python.org/pep-0779/); [What's new in 3.14](https://docs.python.org/3/whatsnew/3.14.html); [NumPy news](https://numpy.org/news/); [NumPy 2.0.0 notes](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/release/2.0.0-notes.html); [IEEE 754-2019](https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/754/6210/); [Python floating-point tutorial](https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/floatingpoint.html); [What's new in 3.12](https://docs.python.org/3/whatsnew/3.12.html); [NEP 19](https://numpy.org/neps/nep-0019-rng-policy.html); [NumPy random compatibility policy](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/random/compatibility.html); [Stack Overflow 2026](https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2026/); [Stack Overflow 2025](https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2025/technology). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-088 · Jupyter notebook reproducibility

- **Study:** J. F. Pimentel, L. Murta, V. Braganholo and J. Freire, "A Large-scale Study about Quality and Reproducibility of Jupyter Notebooks", 16th International Conference on Mining Software Repositories (MSR 2019, Montreal), pp. 507–517, DOI 10.1109/MSR.2019.00077. About 1.4 million notebooks collected from GitHub (≈1.16 million unique, from ≈264,000 repositories).
- **Results (as reported by secondary sources citing the paper):** of 863,878 attempted executions of valid notebooks, 208,323 finished successfully (24.11%), 570,476 failed with an error and 9,982 exceeded the time limit; 4.03% produced the same results as the outputs stored in the file. *Ambiguity:* the paper "Bug Analysis in Jupyter Notebook Projects: An Empirical Study" (ACM, DOI 10.1145/3641539) states the 4.03% as a share of the error-free notebooks; other summaries state it as a share of all runs. The chapter therefore says "about 4%" without a denominator. Main causes reported: missing dependencies, hidden state and out-of-order execution, and unavailable data files [Established for 24.11%; Contested for the 4% denominator].
- **Follow-up:** "Understanding and improving the quality and reproducibility of Jupyter notebooks", *Empirical Software Engineering* (2021), notes that running all notebooks in a shared environment may have underestimated reproducibility.
- **Access note:** the paper PDF (Zenodo, Springer) and the 2022 *Scientific Data* study of research-code execution that summarizes it were not reachable from the build environment; figures were taken from search excerpts of the MSR program, the TOSEM/arXiv Bug Analysis paper and the *Scientific Data* article. See Open issues in PROGRESS.md.
- **Sources:** [MSR 2019 program entry](https://2019.msrconf.org/details/msr-2019-papers/36/A-Large-scale-Study-about-Quality-and-Reproducibility-of-Jupyter-Notebooks); [Bug Analysis in Jupyter Notebook Projects (arXiv 2210.06893)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2210.06893); ["A large-scale study on research code quality and execution", Scientific Data 2022](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41597-022-01143-6). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-089 · Python testing tools: pytest release and usage

- **Current release:** PyPI lists pytest 9.2.0 as the latest version (checked with `pip index versions pytest`, Nov 2026); the build environment runs pytest 9.2.0. pytest 9.0.0 (5 Nov 2025) added built-in support for subtests; 9.1.0 (13 Jun 2026) and 9.1.1 (19 Jun 2026) followed [Established].
- **Usage:** PSF–JetBrains Python Developers Survey 2024 (8th edition, responses collected Oct–Nov 2024), unit-testing frameworks (multiple answers allowed): pytest 53%, unittest 23%, mock 11%, doctest 6%, tox 5%, Hypothesis 4%, nose 2%; 36% reported none. Earlier editions: pytest 51% (2022), 52% (2023) [Industry estimate: self-selected survey].
- **Sources:** [PyPI pytest](https://pypi.org/project/pytest/); [pytest changelog](https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/changelog.html); [pytest 9.1.1 announcement](https://mail.python.org/archives/list/python-announce-list@python.org/thread/P22VYCDQGTL7SNP2XVZNFUMYOMVEPNUS/); [Python Developers Survey 2024](https://lp.jetbrains.com/python-developers-survey-2024/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-090 · Git usage and hash functions

- **Usage:** Stack Overflow Developer Survey 2022, "Version control systems": "No other technology is as widely used as Git" — all respondents (71,379 responses) Git 93.87%; professional developers (53,374) 96.65%. The 2024–2026 surveys no longer ask about version control [Industry estimate, dated 2022].
- **Hash function:** Git's "hash-function-transition" design document: after the SHAttered collision (Google and CWI Amsterdam, announced 23 Feb 2017; NIST notice 24 Feb 2017), "Git v2.13.0 and later subsequently moved to a hardened SHA-1 implementation by default, which isn't vulnerable to the SHAttered attack"; hardened SHA-1 gives the same output as SHA-1 for all known inputs except the two SHAttered PDFs; SHA-256 was chosen as the successor (2018). SHA-256 repositories can be created today with `git init --object-format=sha256` [Established].
- **Transition status:** Git's BreakingChanges document lists changing the default hash for new repositories from SHA-1 to SHA-256 for Git 3.0. As of Nov 2026, search results found no release of Git 3.0 and reported that the SHA-256 default is compiled in only with `WITH_BREAKING_CHANGES`; a developer blog (Oct 2026) reported that GitHub had not announced SHA-256 repository support [Industry estimate; git-scm.com was not reachable from the build environment]. GitLab blog, "GitLab now supports SHA256 repositories" (19 Aug 2024): new projects can be created with SHA-256 under "Experimental settings", for test projects only; SHA-256 and SHA-1 repositories do not interoperate [Established].
- **Sources:** [Stack Overflow 2022 survey](https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2022/); [hash-function-transition (kernel.org mirror)](https://www.kernel.org/pub/software/scm/git/docs/technical/hash-function-transition.html); [Git BreakingChanges](https://git-scm.com/docs/BreakingChanges/2.51.0); [NIST, research results on SHA-1 collisions](https://csrc.nist.gov/news/2017/research-results-on-sha-1-collisions); [GitLab SHA-256 announcement](https://about.gitlab.com/blog/gitlab-now-supports-sha256-repositories/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-091 · Untested analysis and deployment failures

- **Reinhart–Rogoff spreadsheet error:** T. Herndon, M. Ash and R. Pollin, "Does High Public Debt Consistently Stifle Economic Growth? A Critique of Reinhart and Rogoff", PERI working paper (Apr 2013), published in the *Cambridge Journal of Economics* (2014). A coding error in the averaging formula of the original spreadsheet omitted five countries (Australia, Austria, Belgium, Canada, Denmark); with that error, selective exclusion of available years and an unconventional weighting corrected, average real GDP growth for country-years with public debt above 90% of GDP was about 2.2% rather than the published −0.1%. Reinhart and Rogoff acknowledged the spreadsheet error and disputed the significance of the other points [Established].
- **Knight Capital:** SEC Release 2013-222 and the order of 16 Oct 2013 (Knight Capital Americas LLC): on 1 Aug 2012, new router code had been deployed to seven of eight SMARS servers; the eighth still contained retired "Power Peg" code, which a repurposed flag reactivated. In the first 45 minutes after the open the router sent more than 4 million orders while trying to fill 212 customer orders; Knight lost over $460 million. Knight paid a $12 million penalty under the market access rule (15c3-5) [Established].
- **Sources:** [Pollin, LSE blog](https://blogs.lse.ac.uk/politicsandpolicy/public-debt-gdp-growth-and-austerity-why-reinhart-and-rogoff-are-wrong); [Retraction Watch, 18 Apr 2013](https://retractionwatch.com/2013/04/18/influential-reinhart-rogoff-economics-paper-suffers-database-error/); [SEC press release 2013-222](https://www.sec.gov/news/press-release/2013-222). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-092 · C language standards, coding standards and embedded usage

- **C23:** ISO/IEC 9899:2024, fifth edition, published October 2024 (catalog: "Publication Date 30-Oct-2024"; completion 31 Oct 2024), 758 pages; "cancels and replaces the fourth edition (ISO/IEC 9899:2018)"; main changes in Annex M. GCC: "GCC has support for ISO C23, the 2023 revision of the ISO C standard (published in 2024). C23 mode is the default since GCC 15"; GCC 15 release notes: default changed "from -std=gnu17 to -std=gnu23", `__STDC_VERSION__` 202311L; GCC 15.1 released 25 Apr 2025 [Established].
- **Two's complement:** GCC manual §4.5: "C23 requires signed integer types to be two's complement"; Clang C status: "Two's complement sign representation N2412 Clang 14"; cppreference (C23, removed features): "Representations for signed integers other than two's complement" [Established].
- **Headline C23 features:** `nullptr` (N3042; GCC 13, Clang 17), `constexpr` objects (N3018; GCC 13, Clang 19), `#embed` (N3017; GCC 15, Clang 19), `_BitInt(N)` (N2763 et al.; GCC 14 on IA-32, x86-64 and AArch64, Clang 15), `typeof` (N2927/N2930; GCC 13, Clang 16); `bool`, `true`, `false`, `alignas`, `alignof`, `static_assert`, `thread_local` become keywords; binary constants, digit separators, `= {}` initialization; K&R function definitions removed [Established].
- **MISRA C:** misra.org.uk: "MISRA C:2025 was published in March 2025, providing an incremental update to the guidance, maintaining support to the C11 and C18 (and earlier) versions of the C language"; MISRA began with vehicle manufacturers and suppliers, first guidelines November 1994, MISRA C launched 1998; "MISRA C:2025 – Addendum 6 … to assess the applicability of the MISRA C guidelines to the Rust programming language" [Established]. Dynamic memory: MISRA C:2012 and C:2023 Directive 4.12, "Dynamic memory allocation shall not be used", and Rule 21.3, which bans the memory allocation and deallocation functions of `<stdlib.h>` (`malloc`, `calloc`, `realloc`, `aligned_alloc`, `free`), both category Required; and Rule 17.2, "Functions shall not call themselves, either directly or indirectly" (Required, 2012 and 2023), all as summarized in MathWorks Polyspace rule documentation (the MISRA text itself is copyrighted and was not read; C:2025's numbering not confirmed) [Established, secondary].
- **CERT C:** SEI, "SEI CERT C Coding Standard: Rules for Developing Safe, Reliable, and Secure Systems (2016 Edition)", 30 Jun 2016; the wiki is "a work in progress" [Established].
- **Usage:** Eclipse Foundation 2024 IoT & Embedded Developer Survey (press release 3 Dec 2024; "nearly 750" respondents), as summarized by I Programmer (4 Dec 2024): "C (55%), C++ (36%), Python (32%), and Java (29%) are the top programming languages for constrained devices". Full report gated; numbers secondary [Industry estimate]. Stack Overflow usage of C among all respondents: 22% (2025), 21.8% (2026); see R-087 [Industry estimate].
- **Sanitizer cost:** Clang documentation, "AddressSanitizer": "Typical slowdown introduced by AddressSanitizer is 2x"; on memory, it uses more than a native run and the "exact overhead depends on the allocations sizes. The smaller the allocations you make the bigger the overhead is" (no single multiplier given). GCC's `-fno-sanitize-recover` documentation: by default UBSan tries to continue after reporting an error; `-fno-sanitize-recover=all` makes every sanitizer abort at the first error [Established].
- **Sources:** [ISO/IEC 9899:2024 catalog (iTeh)](https://standards.iteh.ai/catalog/standards/iso/7b17ee8c-60d9-4a91-aafd-89f3037b1753/iso-iec-9899-2024); [GCC C status](https://gcc.gnu.org/projects/c-status.html); [GCC 15 changes](https://gcc.gnu.org/gcc-15/changes.html); [GCC integers implementation](https://gcc.gnu.org/onlinedocs/gcc/Integers-implementation.html); [Clang C status](https://clang.llvm.org/c_status.html); [MISRA C](https://misra.org.uk/misra-c/); [SEI CERT C 2016](https://www.sei.cmu.edu/library/sei-cert-c-coding-standard-rules-for-developing-safe-reliable-and-secure-systems-2016-edition/); [Eclipse press release](https://newsroom.eclipse.org/node/41237); [I Programmer summary](https://i-programmer.info/news/91-hardware/17663-eclipse-iot-developer-survey-2024.html); [Clang AddressSanitizer](https://clang.llvm.org/docs/AddressSanitizer.html); [GCC instrumentation options](https://gcc.gnu.org/onlinedocs/gcc/Instrumentation-Options.html); [Polyspace, MISRA C:2023 Dir 4.12](https://www.mathworks.com/help/bugfinder/ref/misrac2023d4.12.html); [Polyspace, MISRA C:2023 Rule 21.3](https://www.mathworks.com/help/bugfinder/ref/misrac2023rule21.3.html); [Polyspace, MISRA C:2012 Rule 17.2](https://www.mathworks.com/help/bugfinder/ref/misrac2012rule17.2.html). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-093 · Software failures from numeric conversions and counters

- **Ariane 5 Flight 501 (4 Jun 1996):** Inquiry Board report (Lions, 19 Jul 1996): "The internal SRI software exception was caused during execution of a data conversion from 64-bit floating point to 16-bit signed integer value. The floating point number which was converted had a value greater than what could be represented by a 16-bit signed integer. This resulted in an Operand Error. The data conversion instructions (in Ada code) were not protected"; seven variables were at risk, four protected, three not, partly because of an 80% workload target; the value (horizontal bias, BH) was high because Ariane 5's early trajectory differs from Ariane 4's; "complete loss of guidance and attitude information 37 seconds after start of the main engine ignition sequence (30 seconds after lift-off)" [Established].
- **Boeing 787 generator control units:** FAA Airworthiness Directive 2015-09-07 (Federal Register, 1 May 2015): "a Model 787 airplane that has been powered continuously for 248 days can lose all alternating current (AC) electrical power due to the generator control units (GCUs) simultaneously going into failsafe mode. This condition is caused by a software counter internal to the GCUs that will overflow after 248 days of continuous power"; required action: deactivate electrical power at intervals not exceeding 120 days [Established]. The AD gives neither the counter width nor the tick period; $2^{31} \times 10$ ms = 248.55 days is our consistent inference only, not a sourced fact (unconfirmed).
- **Patriot (Dhahran, 25 Feb 1991):** see R-057.
- **Sources:** [Ariane 501 Inquiry Board report (ESA, archived)](https://web.archive.org/web/2000id_/http://www.esrin.esa.it/htdocs/tidc/Press/Press96/ariane5rep.html); [ESA media advisory 33-1996](https://www.esa.int/Newsroom/Press_Releases/Ariane_501_-_Presentation_of_Inquiry_Board_report); [FAA AD 2015-09-07 (govinfo)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2015-05-01/html/2015-10066.htm). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-094 · Memory-safety vulnerability data and policy

- **Microsoft:** MSRC Team, "A proactive approach to more secure code", MSRC blog, 16 Jul 2019: "the majority of vulnerabilities fixed and with a CVE assigned are caused by developers inadvertently inserting memory corruption bugs into their C and C++ code"; figure caption "~70% of the vulnerabilities Microsoft assigns a CVE each year continue to be memory safety issues". CISA dates the data to 2006–2018 CVEs [Industry estimate: vendor self-report].
- **Chromium:** The Chromium Projects, "Memory safety" (living page, read Nov 2026): "Around 70% of our high severity security bugs are memory unsafety problems (that is, mistakes with C/C++ pointers). Half of those are use-after-free bugs. (Analysis based on 912 high or critical severity security bugs since 2015, affecting the Stable channel.)" [Industry estimate].
- **CISA et al.:** "The Case for Memory Safe Roadmaps" (CISA, NSA, FBI, ASD's ACSC, Canadian Centre for Cyber Security, NCSC-UK, NCSC-NZ, CERT NZ; December 2023): cites the Microsoft and Chromium 70% figures, "32 of 34 critical/high bugs" at Mozilla, and "67 percent of zero-day vulnerabilities in 2021" (Google Project Zero); "two-thirds of reported vulnerabilities in memory unsafe programming languages still relate to memory issues" [Established: government guidance compiling industry data].
- **White House ONCD:** "Back to the Building Blocks: A Path Toward Secure and Measurable Software", February 2024: "up to 70 percent of security vulnerabilities in memory unsafe languages patched and assigned a CVE designation are due to memory safety issues"; notes C and C++ remain the most widely used languages for space and embedded systems and that Rust "has not yet been proven in space systems"; cites hardware approaches: Arm's memory-tagging extension (MTE) and CHERI capabilities. Archived document of the previous administration [Established].
- **Sources:** [MSRC blog (archived)](https://web.archive.org/web/2019/https://msrc-blog.microsoft.com/2019/07/16/a-proactive-approach-to-more-secure-code/); [Chromium memory safety](https://www.chromium.org/Home/chromium-security/memory-safety/); [CISA, The Case for Memory Safe Roadmaps (archived PDF)](https://web.archive.org/web/2024id_/https://www.cisa.gov/sites/default/files/2023-12/The-Case-for-Memory-Safe-Roadmaps-508c.pdf); [ONCD technical report](https://bidenwhitehouse.archives.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/Final-ONCD-Technical-Report.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-095 · Rust adoption in Android and the Linux kernel

- **Android:** J. Vander Stoep and A. Rebert, "Eliminating Memory Safety Vulnerabilities at the Source", Google Online Security Blog, 25 Sep 2024: memory-safety issues "accounted for 76% of Android vulnerabilities in 2019, and are currently 24% in 2024" (2024 partly extrapolated); "vulnerabilities decay exponentially. They have a half-life." J. Vander Stoep, "Rust in Android: move fast and fix things", 13 Nov 2025: "memory safety vulnerabilities falling below 20% of total vulnerabilities for the first time"; "a 1000x reduction in memory safety vulnerability density compared to Android's C and C++ code"; basis: "roughly 5 million lines of Rust in the Android platform and one potential memory safety vulnerability found (and fixed pre-release), our estimated vulnerability density for Rust is 0.2 vuln per 1 million lines … C and C++ … closer to 1,000 memory safety vulnerabilities per MLOC"; Rust changes have "~4x lower" rollback rates for medium and large changes [Industry estimate: vendor data; the Rust density rests on n = 1].
- **Linux kernel:** KernelNewbies, Linux 6.1 (released 11 Dec 2022): "initial support for the Rust programming language". J. Corbet, "The (successful) end of the kernel Rust experiment", LWN.net, 10 Dec 2025: "Rust in the kernel is no longer experimental — it is now a core part of the kernel and is here to stay." Kernel documentation, "Arch Support": rustc "uses LLVM for code generation, which limits the supported architectures"; arm and arm64 little-endian only [Established].
- **Sources:** [Google Security Blog, Sep 2024](https://security.googleblog.com/2024/09/eliminating-memory-safety-vulnerabilities-Android.html); [Google, Nov 2025](https://security.googleblog.com/2025/11/rust-in-android-move-fast-fix-things.html); [KernelNewbies Linux 6.1](https://kernelnewbies.org/Linux_6.1); [LWN.net](https://lwn.net/Articles/1049831/); [kernel Rust arch support](https://docs.kernel.org/rust/arch-support.html). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-096 · Processor byte order: Arm Cortex-M and RISC-V

- **Arm Cortex-M:** Arm, *Cortex-M4 Devices Generic User Guide*, ARM DUI 0553A (16 Dec 2010): "The memory endianness used is implementation-defined, and the following subsections describe the possible implementations: • Byte-invariant big-endian format • Little-endian format. Read the AIRCR.ENDIANNESS field to find the implemented endianness"; "Instruction memory and Private Peripheral Bus (PPB) accesses are always performed as little-endian." STMicroelectronics, PM0214 (STM32F3/F4/L4/L4+ Cortex-M4 programming manual), Rev 6, Oct 2017: "manages all memory accesses as little-endian." [Established]
- **RISC-V:** *The RISC-V Instruction Set Manual*, ratified release v20260120, unprivileged "Introduction": "RISC-V base ISAs have either little-endian or big-endian memory systems, with the privileged architecture further defining bi-endian operation. Instructions are stored in memory as a sequence of 16-bit little-endian parcels, regardless of memory system endianness"; commentary: little-endian was originally chosen "because little-endian systems are currently dominant commercially (all x86 systems; iOS, Android, and Windows for ARM)", with big- and bi-endian variants defined for networking and legacy code. Privileged spec: "Instruction fetches are always little-endian." [Established]
- **x86:** little-endian (as the RISC-V commentary states; also standard architecture documentation) [Established].
- **Sources:** [Arm DUI 0553A](https://documentation-service.arm.com/static/5f2ac4ab60a93e65927bbdbf); [ST PM0214 (course mirror)](https://se203.telecom-paris.fr/tp/docs/stm32l475xx_pm.pdf); [RISC-V unprivileged intro](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/intro.html); [RISC-V machine-level ISA](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/priv/machine.html). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-097 · Safety-standard coverage requirements and the defect-cost curve

- **DO-178C (RTCA, 2011), avionics software:** structural coverage objectives scale with software level: MC/DC for Level A (with independence), decision coverage for Level B, statement coverage for Level C. Confirmed via tool-vendor summaries (Rapita Systems tables; LDRA, "MC/DC": DO-178C "requires MC/DC for the most critical software level (DAL A)"); the standard itself is paywalled and was not read [Established: consistent across sources].
- **ISO 26262-6:2018, automotive software:** the table of structural coverage methods at the unit level rates MC/DC "highly recommended" (++) for ASIL D and "recommended" (+) for ASIL A–C, as reported by Rapita Systems and other vendor summaries; exact cell values should be checked in the standard [Established for the ASIL D rating; secondary sources].
- **MC/DC tutorial:** Hayhurst, Veerhusen, Chilenski and Rierson, *A Practical Tutorial on Modified Condition/Decision Coverage*, NASA/TM-2001-210876 (2001) [Established].
- **Defect-cost escalation curve:** the widely repeated claim that a defect costs 10× to 100× more to fix in each later phase traces to Boehm (1976) and later compilations; L. Bossavit, *The Leprechauns of Software Engineering* (Leanpub), and his TechWell article "What Does It Really Cost to Fix a Software Defect?" (Oct 2013) argue that the underlying evidence is thin, anecdotal and often mis-cited [Contested].
- **Sources:** [Rapita, SIL vs DAL and coverage](https://www.rapitasystems.com/blog/whats-difference-between-sil-and-dal-how-does-it-affect-my-code-coverage); [LDRA, MC/DC](https://ldra.com/capabilities/mc-dc); [Bossavit, Leprechauns (Leanpub)](https://leanpub.com/leprechauns); [TechWell, Oct 2013](https://www.techwell.com/techwell-insights/2013/10/what-does-it-really-cost-fix-software-defect). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-098 · USB Power Delivery, rack power distribution and AI rack power

*Search excerpts only:* usb.org, developer.nvidia.com, iea.org and ti.com could not be opened from the build environment (outside its network allowlist), so the quotes below are the search tool's renderings of the pages, not text read on the pages themselves; tags are capped accordingly and should be upgraded when the pages are re-read (PROGRESS open issue 21).

- **USB PD 3.1 (USB Promoter Group release, Business Wire, 26 May 2021):** "Prior to this update, USB PD was limited to 100W via a solution based on 20V using USB Type-C cables rated at 5A"; usb.org charger page: "New 28V, 36V, and 48V fixed voltages enable up to 140W, 180W and 240W power levels, respectively"; USB Type-C Release 2.1 defines 240 W cables. 48 V × 5 A = 240 W [Industry estimate as read; Established once the specification page is opened]. The current revision may be 3.2 (Jan 2026 USB-IF logo document); the 240 W ceiling is unchanged; revision not verified.
- **OCP Open Rack V3:** Base Specification V3 Rev 1.1 (2024) and connector specifications: a 48 V-class DC busbar, IT-gear input "46.0V to 52.0V DC"; the base spec's options are summarized as 51 V nominal (Option 1) and 54 V (Option 2); power shelves of 3 kW rectifier modules ("48V rectifier module – 3kW … 54.5V nominal" in an OCP overview deck; later HPR V2 documents describe a narrower ≈ 49–50 V setpoint). Setpoints vary by revision and option [Industry estimate].
- **NVIDIA 800 V DC (NVIDIA Technical Blog, May 2025, around Computex; partner releases 20–21 May 2025):** "NVIDIA is leading the transition to 800 VDC data center power infrastructure to support 1 MW IT racks starting in 2027"; "the physics of using 54 V DC in a single 1 MW rack requires up to 200 kg of copper busbar"; efficiency "up to 5% compared to current 54 V systems" (vendor claim). October 2025 follow-up: "the same wire gauge can carry 157% more power than 415 VAC". OCP blog (11 Aug 2026): Google, Microsoft and NVIDIA standardizing ±400 V / 0–800 V DC ("LVDC") distribution with sidecar power racks [Industry estimate: vendor and consortium announcements].
- **AI rack power:** NVIDIA DGX GB200 rack documentation: "The rack power consumption is approximately 120kW"; HPE QuickSpecs for GB200 NVL72: 132 kW nominal, busways provisioned for about 192 kW peak; Supermicro: eight 33 kW power shelves, 125–135 kW operating range. GB300 NVL72: HPE "each rack consumes between 132 kW and 140 kW"; NVIDIA reference architecture up to 142 kW [Contested: 120–142 kW depending on definition (typical, nominal, provisioned)].
- **Not used:** a per-GPU B300 power of "up to 1,400 W" appeared in one search excerpt and could not be reproduced.
- **Sources:** [USB-IF USB Charger (PD)](https://www.usb.org/usb-charger-pd); [USB PD 3.1 release (Business Wire)](https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20210526006131/en/USB-Promoter-Group-Announces-USB-Power-Delivery-Specification-Revision-3.1); [OCP Open Rack V3 base spec](https://www.opencompute.org/documents/open-rack-base-specification-version-3-rev1-1-030524-pdf); [NVIDIA 800 VDC blog](https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-800-v-hvdc-architecture-will-power-the-next-generation-of-ai-factories/); [NVIDIA 800 VDC ecosystem blog](https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/building-the-800-vdc-ecosystem-for-efficient-scalable-ai-factories/); [OCP LVDC blog](https://www.opencompute.org/news/powering-the-next-era-of-ai-how-google-microsoft-and-nvidia-are-standardizing-and-accelerating-the-industry-transition-to-lvdc/); [NVIDIA DGX GB200 hardware](https://docs.nvidia.com/dgx/dgxgb200-user-guide/hardware.html); [HPE GB200 NVL72 QuickSpecs](https://www.hpe.com/us/en/collaterals/collateral.a50009224enw.html); [HPE GB300 NVL72 QuickSpecs](https://www.hpe.com/us/en/collaterals/collateral.a50009244enw.html).
