## R-100 · Low-power MCU currents, coin cells and CPU core-voltage ranges

*Search excerpts only:* usb.org, developer.nvidia.com, iea.org and ti.com could not be opened from the build environment (outside its network allowlist), so the quotes below are the search tool's renderings of the pages, not text read on the pages themselves; tags are capped accordingly and should be upgraded when the pages are re-read (PROGRESS open issue 21).

- **ST STM32U0:** STM32U083 datasheet (DS14463): "Run mode (LDO mode): 52 μA/MHz"; "Stop 2 mode: 825 nA with RTC"; ST blog: "160 nA in standby mode with RTC", 16 nA in Shutdown. The datasheet's CoreMark table gives 70 µA/MHz at 1.8 V and 73 µA/MHz at 3.0 V (48 MHz, from SRAM): the headline is a best case [Industry estimate: vendor typicals at 25 °C].
- **Nordic nRF54L15:** "193 CoreMark/mA (@ 3 V)", 503 CoreMark at 128 MHz (≈ 20 µA/MHz); System OFF "down to 0.8 μA (@ 3.0 V)" with the global RTC; System ON idle 2.9 µA with 256 KB RAM retained [Industry estimate].
- **Ambiq Apollo4 Plus:** "4 μA/MHz executing from MRAM (with cache)"; supply voltage of the figure not captured [Industry estimate].
- **CR2032 coin cells:** Panasonic CR2032 datasheet: 3 V nominal, 225 mAh at 0.2 mA continuous drain; Energizer ECRN2032: "typical capacity of 254 mAh to 2.0 volts, rated at 15K ohms at 21°C" [Industry estimate as read; Established once the datasheets are opened]. The often-quoted 235 mAh figure appears, per a later search excerpt (Course 06 audit, Nov 2026), in Energizer's standard CR2032 datasheet (data.energizer.com/pdfs/cr2032.pdf) as 235 mAh to 2.0 V at 15 kΩ; that PDF could not be opened from the build environment, so the figure is unconfirmed and not used.
- **CPU core rail:** Intel 13th-generation Core datasheet, Vol. 1, VCCCORE DC specifications: allowed range 0 to 1.6 V (U/P/H/PX) and 0 to 1.72 V (HX), a range, not a typical operating voltage; no official GPU core-voltage figure was found (not used in the chapter).
- **Sources:** [ST STM32U083 datasheet](https://www.st.com/resource/en/datasheet/stm32u083kc.pdf); [ST blog, STM32U0](https://blog.st.com/stm32u0/); [Nordic nRF54L15](https://www.nordicsemi.com/Products/nRF54L15); [Ambiq Apollo4 Plus](https://ambiq.com/product/apollo4-plus/); [Panasonic CR2032](https://energy.panasonic.com/dam/master/pdf/en/datasheet/lithium/CR2032_Datasheet_EN.pdf); [Energizer ECRN2032](https://data.energizer.com/pdfs/ecrn2032-industrial-gl0920.pdf); [Intel 13th-gen datasheet VCCCORE](https://edc.intel.com/content/www/us/en/design/products/platforms/details/raptor-lake-s/13th-generation-core-processors-datasheet-volume-1-of-2/vcccore-dc-specifications/). Checked Nov 2026 (search excerpts).

## R-101 · Resistors: preferred values, film technologies, copper and shunts

*Search excerpts only:* the IEC webstore, Vishay, Panasonic, ROHM, NIST and TI pages could not be opened from the build environment, so the quotes below are the search tool's renderings; tags are capped accordingly (PROGRESS open issue 21).

- **IEC 60063:2015,** *Preferred number series for resistors and capacitors*, edition 3.0, published 27 Mar 2015 (IEC TC 40): provides "series of preferred values for the resistance of resistors and for the capacitance of capacitors"; the changes from the previous edition concern the "relationship between an E Series and the tolerance". E3–E24 values are rounded to two significant figures and E48–E192 to three; customary tolerances (E6 ±20% … E96 ±1%) are a convention (secondary source: Wikipedia's summary) [Industry estimate].
- **Thick film (Vishay D/CRCW e3, doc 20035):** "Temperature coefficient ± 200 ppm/K; ± 100 ppm/K"; tolerance ±5% or ±1%; 1 Ω to 10 MΩ; "ΔR/R ≤ 1 % after 1000 h at rated power at 70 °C" [Industry estimate].
- **Thin film (Vishay TNPW e3, doc 28758, revision 10-Apr-2026):** TCR "± 50 ppm/K; ± 25 ppm/K; ± 15 ppm/K; ± 10 ppm/K" by case size; tolerance ±1%, ±0.5%, ±0.1%; TNPU e3 (2020 press release): "down to ± 5 ppm/K and tighter tolerances down to ± 0.02 %" [Industry estimate].
- **Small chip sizes:** Panasonic ERJ-XGN: "0.4 x 0.2 (EIA: 01005)", 0.031 W; ROHM SMR003 (metric 03015, 0.30 × 0.15 mm, 0.02 W) at last-time-buy status; ROHM's 2014 catalog announced a 0.25 × 0.125 mm SMR002 [Contested for "smallest in production"].
- **Copper (IACS):** NBS Handbook 100 and ASTM E1004: 100% IACS is 1.7241 µΩ·cm at 20 °C (σ = 58 MS/m), temperature coefficient 0.00393 /°C (also R-080) [Industry estimate as read; Established in substance].
- **PCB copper:** TI SNVA054C: "1 oz is actually equivalent to 1.4 mils copper thickness (or 35 µm)"; TI SLUA236: "0.0014 inches ±0.0002 inches"; ADI *Basic Linear Design* ch. 12 uses 1.724 µΩ·cm and 0.036 mm (its text writes "Ω/cm" for Ω·cm and calls the 20 °C IACS value a 25 °C value). Derived here: $\rho/t$ ≈ 0.49 mΩ/□ at 20 °C, with ±7–15% spread from plating and etch [Industry estimate].
- **Four-terminal shunt (Vishay WSK2512, doc 30108):** 0.5 mΩ to 0.2 Ω; "4-terminal design allows for 1 % tolerance down to 0.0005 Ω"; TCR ±350 ppm/°C (0.5–0.99 mΩ), ±250 (1–2.9 mΩ), ±75 (3–4.9 mΩ), ±35 ppm/°C (5 mΩ–0.2 Ω), element below 20 ppm/°C. The explanation that copper terminations dominate the TCR at low values is our inference, not the datasheet's [Industry estimate].
- **Sources:** [IEC 60063:2015](https://webstore.iec.ch/en/publication/22011); [Vishay D/CRCW e3](https://www.vishay.com/docs/20035/dcrcwe3.pdf); [Vishay TNPW e3](https://www.vishay.com/docs/28758/tnpw_e3.pdf); [Panasonic ERJ chip resistors](https://industrial.panasonic.com/cdbs/www-data/pdf/RDA0000/AOA0000C301.pdf); [ROHM SMR003](https://www.rohm.com/products/resistors/class-leading-compact-size-chip-resistors/ultra-compact-chip-resistors/smr003rxf-product); [NBS Handbook 100](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/hb/nbshandbook100.pdf); [TI SNVA054](https://www.ti.com/lit/pdf/snva054); [ADI Basic Linear Design ch. 12](https://www.analog.com/media/en/training-seminars/design-handbooks/Basic-Linear-Design/Chapter12.pdf); [Vishay WSK2512](https://www.vishay.com/docs/30108/wsk2512.pdf). Checked Nov 2026 (search excerpts).

## R-102 · Strain gauges: gauge factor and bridge excitation

*Search excerpts only:* the HBM/HBK and Micro-Measurements pages could not be opened from the build environment, so the quotes below are the search tool's renderings; tags are capped accordingly (PROGRESS open issue 21).

- **Gauge factor:** HBM/HBK technical notes: gauge factor "about 2.0 for constantan (Y-Series) and 2.2 for its Modco (M-Series) grids" (summary); "With a gauge factor of 2.0, a strain of 5784µm/m is measured, while with a gauge factor of 2.06, a strain of 5618µm/m is measured" [Industry estimate].
- **Excitation:** HBM tech note 4206: 2.5 V is "a typical excitation voltage for a strain gauge quarter bridge"; HBK QuantumX MX1615B datasheet: "DC quarter-bridge excitation of 0.5; 1; 2.5; 5 (±5 %) volts"; an HBK note's heating example: "52 mW for 5 V RMS into a 120 Ω gauge" (half of the bridge voltage across each gauge: $2.5^2/120$ = 52 mW, our check); Micro-Measurements' reference guide cites 0.5 to 12 V DC [Industry estimate].
- **Derived in the chapter:** ΔR/R = GF·ε, so 1000 µε with GF = 2 gives 0.2%, and a 2.5 V quarter bridge about 1.25 mV.
- **Sources:** [HBM, strain gauge excitation voltage](https://www.hbm.com/en/4206/tips-and-tricks-strain-gauge-excitation-voltage/); [HBM, gauge factor deviation](https://www.hbm.com/en/7942/gauge-factor-deviation/); [HBK strain measurement glossary](https://www.hbkworld.com/en/knowledge/resource-center/articles/strain-measurement-glossary1); [Micro-Measurements reference guide](https://docs.micro-measurements.com/?id=8690). Checked Nov 2026 (search excerpts).

## R-103 · Modified nodal analysis and simulator capacity

- **The MNA paper:** C.-W. Ho, A. E. Ruehli and P. A. Brennan, "The modified nodal approach to network analysis," *IEEE Transactions on Circuits and Systems*, vol. CAS-22, no. 6, pp. 504–509, June 1975, DOI 10.1109/TCS.1975.1084079 (bibliographic data confirmed on Crossref). The authors were at the IBM Thomas J. Watson Research Center, Yorktown Heights. The abstract says the nodal method cannot process "voltage sources and current-dependent circuit elements in a simple and efficient manner", and that MNA "retains the simplicity and other advantages of nodal analysis while removing its limitations". Numerical comparisons with the tableau method favor MNA "in terms of the dimension, number of nonzeros, and fill-ins". Table I gives "element rubber stamps", the origin of the term *stamp* [Established].
- **Commercial simulator capacity (vendor claims):**
  - Cadence describes Spectre X (introduced June 2019) as delivering "up to 10X simulation performance gains for large, post-layout designs" and solving "5X larger designs" than its previous simulators, for "designs with millions of transistors and billions of parasitics".
  - Synopsys says PrimeSim FastSPICE is "proven capable of handling billions of elements in a 3D stacked memory design".
  - No vendor publishes an element-count specification, and no independent benchmark was found [Contested].
- **FastSPICE trade-off:** a Cadence blog on Spectre FX describes FastSPICE as a SPICE-type simulator that makes different trade-offs to produce results much faster, with accuracy as a user-set dial. Patents on FastSPICE partitioning (for example US 8,060,355) describe simplified device models and circuit partitioning, and warn that improper partitioning or oversimplification costs accuracy. An academic study (Bizzarri, Brambilla and Storti Gajani, 2018) reports partitioning that preserves accuracy, but for DC operating points only [Industry estimate].
- **Sources:**
  - [Crossref record for the MNA paper](https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1109/TCS.1975.1084079); the paper's text was read from [an author-uploaded copy](https://spinningnumbers.org/assets/MNA75.pdf).
  - Vendor claims: [Cadence Spectre X](https://www.cadence.com/en_US/home/tools/custom-ic-analog-rf-design/circuit-simulation/spectre-x-simulator.html), [Cadence blog, June 2019](https://community.cadence.com/cadence_blogs_8/b/cic/posts/tales-from-dac-the-new-spectre-simulator-is-here), [Synopsys blog on PrimeSim](https://www.synopsys.com/blogs/chip-design/primesim-continuum-ic-hyperconvergence.html), [Cadence blog on Spectre FX](https://community.cadence.com/cadence_blogs_8/b/breakfast-bytes/posts/spectrefx), [US 8,060,355](https://patents.google.com/patent/US8060355).
  - The vendor and patent pages returned bot-check pages to direct download, so those claims come from search excerpts (PROGRESS open issue 21). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-104 · Compact models and gm/ID design

*Search excerpts only:* si2.org returned a bot-check page and the Cambridge excerpt PDF failed its TLS handshake from the build environment, so the facts below are the search tool's renderings (PROGRESS open issue 21).

- **Compact Model Coalition:** an industry working group run by Si2 that chooses, maintains and promotes standard semiconductor device models; it brings together university developers, simulator vendors, fabless companies, foundries and integrated device manufacturers. Its quality-assurance program aims to make simulations of the standard models uniform across vendors' simulators. CMC members get new standards immediately, and they are released to the public 18 months after the initial release [Industry estimate].
- **BSIM-CMG:** the CMC standard compact model for multi-gate (FinFET) transistors, developed by UC Berkeley's BSIM group. Si2 announced a 2019 release developed "in conjunction with 20 partners", with 25 enhancements and 13 bug fixes. The current version as of Nov 2026 was not confirmed [Industry estimate].
- **gm/ID methodology:** P. G. A. Jespers and B. Murmann, *Systematic Design of Analog CMOS Circuits: Using Pre-Computed Lookup Tables*, Cambridge University Press, 2017 (ISBN 9781107192256). It sizes transistors with precomputed tables of $g_m/I_D$ and related small-signal parameters, organized by inversion level, using script-based flows in place of repeated simulation; MATLAB code accompanies it [Industry estimate].
- **Sources:** [Si2 release on BSIM-CMG](https://si2.org/?p=10490); [Compact Model Coalition (Wikipedia, used only for the access policy)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compact_Model_Coalition); [Cambridge University Press, chapter page](https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/systematic-design-of-analog-cmos-circuits/basic-sizing-using-the-gmid-methodology/185BE8CB4F04AD9FFC08CDE06D3EA8A9). Checked Nov 2026 (search excerpts).

## R-105 · Thevenin and Norton history and Power over Ethernet

- **Power over Ethernet (read in full):** Ethernet Alliance, *Overview of 802.3bt Power over Ethernet*, December 2019, based on the published IEEE Std 802.3bt-2018. It gives:
  - Types and classes: PSE power 4, 7, 15.4, 30, 45, 60, 75 and 90 W for Classes 1–8, and PD power 3.84, 6.49, 13, 25.5, 40, 51, 62 and 71.3 W. 802.3af (2003) provided 13 W to devices and 802.3at raised this to 25.5 W.
  - Type 3 supports up to 51 W at the PD and Type 4 up to 71.3 W; Type 4 is always 4-pair.
  - Minimum PSE output voltage: Type 1 44 V, Type 2 50 V, Type 3 50 V, Type 4 52 V. The maximum for all PSEs is 57 V.
  - Channel resistance: the worst-case pairset loop resistance R_Ch is 12.5 Ω, or 6.25 Ω with two pairsets in parallel (4-pair); Type 1 PDs must support 20 Ω, giving "a voltage as low as 37 V".
  - Minimum PD input voltage table, Type 4 column: Class 8 43.0 V; Type 2/3 columns: Class 4 42.5 V, Class 6 42.5 V.
  - [Established]
- **Our consistency check:** a Thévenin model (minimum PSE voltage behind the worst-case loop resistance, delivering the class's PSE power) reproduces the PD powers of Types 1–4 and the PD voltages of Types 1–3. For Type 4 Class 8 it gives 41.18 V against the table's 43.0 V, a difference not resolved here (the standard presumably defines that minimum under conditions the simple model omits).
- **Thévenin and Norton history:**
  - D. H. Johnson, "Origins of the equivalent circuit concept: the voltage-source equivalent," *Proc. IEEE* 91(4):636–640, April 2003, DOI 10.1109/JPROC.2003.811716.
  - D. H. Johnson, "Origins of the equivalent circuit concept: the current-source equivalent," *Proc. IEEE* 91(5):817–821, May 2003, DOI 10.1109/JPROC.2003.811795.
  - Bibliographic data for both articles were confirmed on Crossref [Established].
  - From search excerpts summarizing the articles: Helmholtz stated the voltage-source equivalent in 1853. Thévenin published it in 1883 (*Comptes Rendus* 97:159–161). Norton described the current-source form in a Bell Labs technical report dated 3 November 1926. H. F. Mayer of Siemens published it independently in 1926 [Industry estimate]: the articles' text was not read, and the original publications were not seen.
- **Sources:** [Ethernet Alliance 802.3bt overview (PDF)](https://ethernetalliance.org/wp-content/uploads/2019/12/WP_EA_Overview8023bt_V2p1_FINAL.pdf); [Crossref: voltage-source article](https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1109/JPROC.2003.811716); [Crossref: current-source article](https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1109/JPROC.2003.811795). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-106 · The 50 ohm standard

*Search excerpts only* for the history: the Microwaves101 page was not returned, and the trade articles below were read only as search-result summaries (PROGRESS open issue 21).

- **Claims in trade and secondary sources:**
  - For air-dielectric coax, "the geometry that provides the greatest peak-power capability corresponds to roughly 30 Ω, while the geometry that minimizes conductor loss corresponds to approximately 77 Ω".
  - 50 Ω is presented as a compromise; one account gives it as "sqrt(77 x 30) = 48.5 ohms, rounds to 50 ohms".
  - With solid polyethylene dielectric, the minimum-loss impedance "shifts to about 51 ohms".
  - 75 Ω (video, cable TV) is said to favor low loss.
  - The attribution of the choice to specific 1930s work was not verified [Industry estimate].
- **Our derivation:**
  - Minimizing $(1 + x)/\ln x$ with $x = b/a$ gives $\ln x = 1 + 1/x$, $x = 3.591$, $Z_0 = 60\ln x = 76.7$ Ω in air.
  - Maximizing $\ln x/x^2$ gives $x = \sqrt e$, $Z_0 = 30.0$ Ω.
  - The geometric mean of the optima is 47.97 Ω (48.06 Ω with 77 Ω), **not 48.5 Ω**: the trade source's arithmetic is wrong.
  - Polyethylene ($\varepsilon_r$ = 2.25) gives 51.1 Ω for minimum loss, confirming the "about 51 ohms" claim.
  - The physics is standard transmission-line theory (Pozar, *Microwave Engineering*) [Established].
- **Sources:** [Hackaday, "What's Special About Fifty Ohms" (11 Jul 2016)](https://hackaday.com/2016/07/11/whats-special-about-fifty-ohms); [Altium, "Mysterious 50 Ohm Impedance"](https://resources.altium.com/p/mysterious-50-ohm-impedance-where-it-came-and-why-we-use-it). Checked Nov 2026 (search excerpts).

*Primary documents read in full* (datasheet electrical-characteristics tables and the companies' SEC filings), Nov 2026.

- **TI OPA187 (datasheet SBOS807E, Dec 2016, revised May 2020)** [Established]:
  - 36 V zero-drift op-amp.
  - At 25 °C: $V_{OS}$ ±1 µV typical, ±10 µV maximum; drift 0.001 µV/°C typical (0.015 µV/°C maximum).
  - $I_B$ ±100 pA typical, ±350 pA maximum.
  - Open-loop gain 160 dB typical; gain-bandwidth 550 kHz; differential input impedance 100 MΩ ∥ 6 pF; quiescent current 100 µA per amplifier (typical).
- **TI LM358 family (datasheet SLOS068AB, June 1976, revised Oct 2024)** [Established]:
  - LM358 at 25 °C: $V_{OS}$ 3 mV typical, 7 mV maximum; $I_B$ −20 nA typical, −250 nA maximum (the minus sign: current flows out of the PNP inputs); $I_{OS}$ 2 nA typical, 50 nA maximum; gain-bandwidth 0.7 MHz.
  - LM358B: $V_{OS}$ ±0.3 mV typical, ±3.0 mV maximum; $I_B$ −10 nA typical, −35 nA maximum; gain-bandwidth 1.2 MHz.

  - By end market: industrial 45%, automotive 30%, consumer 13%, communications 13% (rounded; they sum to 101%).
- **Not found:** a reliable public figure for op-amp market share. Company segment revenues include power management, data converters and much else, so they are not op-amp revenues.

## R-108 · Digital multimeter specifications

*Mixed access:* Fluke's 87V product page and the original Fluke 87 users manual were read in full; Keysight's 34465A documents could not be opened from the build environment, so its figures are from search excerpts only.

- **Fluke 87V (product page, specifications table, read Nov 2026)** [Established]:
  - "Voltage DC ... Accuracy ±(0.05% + 1)"; maximum resolution 10 µV; maximum voltage 1000 V.
  - The page does not state the 87V's DC input impedance.
- **Fluke 87 True RMS Multimeter Users Manual (PN 834192, August 1988, rev. 8, 4/97)** [Established] for the original 87, not the 87V:
  - "Each of the five ac/dc voltage ranges presents an input impedance of approximately 10 MΩ in parallel with less than 100 pF."
  - Loading: "In most cases, the error is negligible (0.1% or less) if the measurement circuit source impedance is 10 kilohms or less."
  - A power-on option changes the 400 mV range's input impedance "from 10 megohms to greater than 4000 megohms".
  - Accuracy is specified "at 18°C to 28°C, with relative humidity up to 90%, for a period of one year after calibration"; DC volts on the 4 V to 400 V ranges ±(0.1% + 1).

  - The 100 mV to 10 V ranges "can be set to either 10 MΩ or 10 GΩ", while the 100 V and 1000 V ranges "remain at a 10 MΩ input impedance".
  - The per-range specification (% of reading + % of range) was not verified and is not used.

## R-109 · PCB copper, wire gauge and four-terminal shunts

*Primary documents read in full* (TI application report and Vishay datasheet), Nov 2026. The AWG definition is standard; ASTM B258 itself was not opened.

- **TI AN-1229, *SIMPLE SWITCHER PCB Layout Guidelines* (SNVA054C, June 2002, revised April 2013)** [Established]:
  - "1 oz is actually equivalent to 1.4 mils copper thickness (or 35 µm)."
  - "A 0.5 cm wide Cu trace of thickness 1.4 mil has a resistance of 1 milliohm per cm length (at 20 ºC)."
  - Our check: 2 squares per centimeter × 0.485 mΩ/□ (IACS copper, 1.4 mil = 35.56 µm) = 0.97 mΩ, consistent with the rounded 1 mΩ.
- **Vishay Dale WSK2512, *Power Metal Strip Resistors, Low Value, Surface-Mount, 4-Terminal* (document 30108, revision 11 Dec 2023)** [Established]:
  - 1.0 W at 70 °C.
  - Resistance ranges by tolerance: ±0.1% for 0.01–0.2 Ω, ±0.5% for 0.001–0.2 Ω, ±1% for 0.0005–0.2 Ω.
  - Temperature coefficient "± 350 for 0.5 mΩ to 0.99 mΩ, ± 250 for 0.001 Ω to 0.0029 Ω, ± 75 for 0.003 Ω to 0.0049 Ω, ± 35 for 0.005 Ω to 0.2 Ω" ppm/°C.
  - Operating range −65 to +170 °C; element alloy (nickel-chrome or manganese-copper) "low TCR (< 20 ppm/°C)"; inductance 0.5 nH to 5 nH.
  - This supersedes R-101's search-excerpt reading of the same datasheet. The copper-termination explanation of the TCR banding remains our inference.
- **American Wire Gauge** [Established]:
  - Diameter $d_n$ = 0.005 in × 92^((36 − n)/39), defined by 36 AWG = 0.005 in and 0000 AWG = 0.46 in with 39 geometric steps between them (ASTM B258; not opened).
  - Our computation: 16 AWG = 1.291 mm (13.17 mΩ/m) and 14 AWG = 1.628 mm (8.285 mΩ/m) with IACS copper at 20 °C.
- **Sources:**
  - [TI SNVA054C](https://www.ti.com/lit/pdf/snva054).
  - [Vishay WSK2512 datasheet](https://www.vishay.com/docs/30108/wsk2512.pdf).
  - Checked Nov 2026.

## R-110 · MLCCs: DC bias, temperature codes, counts and market share

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0701 to F-0707), primary pages opened unless stated.* Extends R-079.

- **DC-bias loss (Samsung Electro-Mechanics engineer, *Electronic Design*, 2 Jul 2020)** [Established]:
  - "the effective capacitance of a 1-µF, 6.3-V-rated X5R MLCC can be reduced to 0.36 µF by applying 3 V dc (47% of the rated voltage)… a 64% reduction". Measured at 500 mV rms, 1 kHz.
  - Aging under DC bias reduces capacitance further and is "not a spec that can typically be found in an MLCC datasheet".
  - The case size is not stated, and the loss depends strongly on case size and dielectric thickness; it is one part's datum, not a rule.
- **EIA temperature codes (Murata part-numbering guide, PDF created 24 Sep 2026)** [Established]:
  - C0G 0 ± 30 ppm/°C (−55 to 125 °C); X5R ±15% (−55 to 85 °C); X7R ±15% (−55 to 125 °C); X8R ±15% (−55 to 150 °C); X6S ±22% (−55 to 105 °C); X7T +22/−33%; X7U +22/−56%.
  - Code: first letter is the low-temperature limit, digit the high limit, last letter the maximum change. EIA-198 itself was not opened.
- **EIA RS-198 classes (KEMET catalog, undated)** [Established]: Class I "not affected by voltage, frequency or time"; Class II within ±15% and "affected by temperature, voltage, frequency and time"; Class III, for example Z5U +22/−56% over +10 to +85 °C.
- **Counts (Murata article, 23 Jun 2021)** [Industry estimate]: "as many as 3,000 to 5,000 MLCCs are utilized per vehicle"; "even the latest high-end [smartphone] model includes 1,000 MLCCs"; some Level 2+ BEVs use "more than 10,000".
- **AI servers (DIGITIMES Asia, 12 Jun 2025, citing SK Securities)** [Industry estimate]: about eight times more MLCCs than traditional servers; "Some estimates suggest up to 25,000 MLCCs per AI server versus 2,000 in conventional units" (unattributed estimate).
- **Market share** [Contested]:
  - Murata's corporate page: "Murata's share of the MLCC market is 40%" (undated; basis not stated).
  - DIGITIMES: Samsung Electro-Mechanics "captured 24% of the global market" in 1H 2024, second to Murata, but the same article also places it in a "10% to 20%" group.
  - Search excerpts (not opened) cite Yageo's annual report at Murata "over 30%" in 2024.
  - Range taught: Murata about 30–40%, Samsung Electro-Mechanics about 10–25% (widened after the Course 07 audit to cover DIGITIMES's own 10–20% grouping).
- **Not verified:** the Maxim/ADI Tutorial 5527 example (4.7 µF, 6.3 V, 0603 X5R about 93% below nominal at 5 V) remains a search excerpt (analog.com unreachable) and is not used.
- **Sources:**

## R-111 · Electrolytic life, supercapacitors, inductor saturation and silicon capacitors

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0708 to F-0714), primary documents opened unless stated.* Extends R-082.

- **Aluminum electrolytic life (Nippon Chemi-Con technical note, CAT. No. E1001A 2026)** [Established]:
  - Life is limited mainly by "loss of electrolyte … through the rubber seal", following "the Arrhenius' Law"; "Lx = Lo·Bt^((To−Tx)/10)" with "BT … approximately 2 over an ambient temperature range from 60°C to 95°C", so "the lifetime is approximately halved for every 10°C rise".
  - For parts rated 105 °C or higher, "the temperature acceleration factor (BT) needs to be modified".
- **A low-impedance 105 °C series (Chemi-Con KYB sheet, 2026)** [Established]: "Endurance with ripple current: 8,000/10,000 hours at 105℃" (6.3–10 V / 16–100 V), end of life at a capacitance change of 30%/25% or D.F. at 200% of the initial value: life ends at a parametric limit.
- **Supercapacitor (Maxwell K2 3.0 V/3000 F datasheet, document 3001423-EN.1, Apr 2016, distributor copy)** [Established]: 3.00 V, 3,000 F, ESR 0.27 mΩ, "Specific Energy, Emax 7.2 Wh/kg", "Stored Energy 3.75 Wh", 520 g. Check: ½ × 3000 × 3² = 13,500 J = 3.75 Wh.
- **Inductor saturation current definitions:**
  - Vishay IHLP-4040DZ-01 (document 34193, revision 02-Oct-2026): "DC current (A) that will cause L to drop approximately 20 %"; the 1.0 µH part: Isat 36.0 A, heat rating 17.5 A (ΔT ≈ 40 °C), DCR 3.70 mΩ typical [Established].
  - Coilcraft XAL7070: 30% drop (R-082) [Established].
  - Würth WE-XHMI 74439370330: "ISAT 1 |ΔL/L| < 10 % 6.8 A typ." and "ISAT 2 |ΔL/L| < 30 % 18.15 A typ." [Industry estimate] (search excerpt; we-online.com denied access).
- **Silicon capacitors:**
  - Murata release (Jul 2021): "silicon capacitors with a density of 1.3µF/mm²", ESL "few pH", profile "<40µm" [Industry estimate] (vendor claim, page read); Murata Hot Chips 2025 poster targets ">1 uF/mm2" integrated passives.
  - TSMC, S. Y. Hou et al., "Integrated Deep Trench Capacitor in Si Interposer for CoWoS Heterogeneous Integration", IEDM 2019, doi 10.1109/IEDM19573.2019.8993498 (metadata confirmed via Crossref): "up to 340 nF/mm²" and "up to 68 µF per interposer die" [Industry estimate] (abstract read only as a search excerpt).
- **Sources:**
  - [Chemi-Con technical note](https://www.chemi-con.co.jp/products/relatedfiles/capacitor/catalog/al-technote-e.pdf).
  - [Chemi-Con KYB series](https://www.chemi-con.co.jp/products/relatedfiles/capacitor/catalog/KYBLL-e.PDF).
  - [Maxwell BCAP3000 datasheet (distributor copy)](https://www.boser-tech.com/pdf-9a/BCAP3000-P300-K04.pdf).
  - [Vishay IHLP-4040DZ-01](https://www.vishay.com/docs/34193/ihlp-4040dz-01.pdf).
  - [Würth WE-XHMI](https://www.we-online.com/en/components/products/WE-XHMI) (not opened).
  - [Murata silicon capacitor release](https://www.murata.com/news/capacitor/siliconcapacitors/2021/0618).

## R-112 · Hot-plug overshoot with ceramic input capacitors

*Gathered by a research subagent (07-03 facts file, entries F-0736 to F-0740); primary documents opened unless stated.*

- **Mechanism and the 2× bound (G. Perica, Linear Technology, "Ceramic capacitors in dc/dc-input filters: OK, but watch out for those transients", *EDN*, 10 May 2001, pp. 107–114, the magazine version of LT Application Note 88)** [Established]:
  - "RIN, LEQ, CIN, and REQ constitute a series-resonant tank."
  - "In a lossless linear circuit, the peak voltage would reach 2•VIN. With losses, the peak voltage is less than 2•VIN."
  - "With the nonlinear Y5V capacitor, the peak voltage reaches 3•VIN, showing the effect of the significantly lower capacitance at the higher bias voltage."
  - Bench: a 24 V laptop adapter (1000 µF output, 1–10 µH output inductor, 3–10 ft cable) into a 10 µF ceramic peaked at 57.2 V. With a 22 µF ceramic, the fixes gave (Table 4): none 40.8 V; extra 22 µF ceramic with 0.5 Ω in series 30 V; tantalum 33 V; 30 V TVS 35 V (Table 3 lists the TVS case at 32 V, an inconsistency in the article); a 47 µF, 35 V aluminum electrolytic with 0.44 Ω ESR 25 V, described as "critical damping".
  - Our arithmetic, not the article's: with the stated 1–10 µH and the 22 µF ceramic, $Z_0 = \sqrt{L/C}$ spans 0.21–0.67 Ω, which brackets the 0.44 Ω ESR. (An earlier version compared the ESR with $2Z_0$ for an assumed 1 µH; the course's damper rule is $R_d \approx Z_0$, and the article does not give a single inductance.)
  - LT Application Note 88 itself (March 2001) could not be opened (analog.com outside the build environment's network); its title, author and date are from search excerpts.
- **LT3684 datasheet, Rev A, p. 16, "Hot Plugging Safely"** (text read from a third-party mirror; Figure 9 not seen) [Established]: the ceramic input capacitor and the source's stray inductance form "an under damped tank circuit, and the voltage at the VIN pin … can ring to twice the nominal input voltage"; a 24 V supply through six feet of 24-gauge twisted pair into 4.7 µF "rings as high as 50V and the input current peaks at 26A"; "A 0.7Ω resistor is added in series with the input to eliminate the voltage overshoot".
- **onsemi AND90059/D, Rev 1, Aug 2021, "Understanding eFuse Input Voltage Transients from Hot Plug Events"** [Established]: the hot plug is "a Simplified Series RLC Circuit"; case 1 (30 V, 0.1 Ω, 0.5 µH, 1 µF) peaks at 54 V (80% surge) and 38 A; case 7 (5 µH) 58 V and 13 A; "the max capacitor voltage (Max Vc) is never more than twice the supply voltage"; a 12-inch 18-gauge cable with the supply measured 500 nH and 120 mΩ; a 33 V Zener cut the peak from 54 V to 48 V.
- **TI SLUAAL8, May 2023, "How to Solve Voltage Overshoot While Hot Plugging"** (E. Galloway) [Established]: damping condition "Ri + Rc > 2 √(Li/Ci)"; recommends an RC snubber (2 Ω, 0.5 W anti-surge resistor with 2.2 µF, plus 0.1 µF at the pin) rather than relying on the IC pin's capacitor; a TVS or Zener as a costlier alternative.
- **Sources:**
  - [EDN article, archive copy](https://www.pe2bz.philpem.me.uk/Comm01/-%20-%20Parts-NonActive/Capacitor/82784.pdf).
  - [LT AN88](https://www.analog.com/media/en/technical-documentation/application-notes/an88f.pdf) (not opened).
  - [LT3684 datasheet pages, mirror](https://www.omo-ic.com/pdf/364574/LT3684EDD-TRPBF/P16-P18.html).
  - [onsemi AND90059/D](https://www.onsemi.com/pub/Collateral/AND90059-D.PDF).
  - [TI SLUAAL8](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/sluaal8/sluaal8.pdf).
  - Checked Nov 2026.

## R-113 · RC snubbers and gate-drive damping

*Gathered by a research subagent (07-03 facts file, entries F-0741 to F-0743 and F-0750).*

- **P. C. Todd, "Snubber Circuits: Theory, Design and Application", Unitrode Power Supply Design Seminar SEM-900, Topic 2, May 1993 (TI SLUP100; read from a TI E2E-hosted copy, the official URL requiring a TI login)** [Established]:
  - "the value of the resistor must be close to the impedance of the parasitic resonance which it is intended to damp"; "The snubber capacitance will generally be two to four times" the switch's output capacitance.
  - "Increase the value of the capacitor until the frequency of the ringing to be damped has been halved … the additional capacitance is three times the original circuit value. This is a near optimum value for the capacitor since it allows damping very near Q = 1."
  - Example: 2 µH leakage inductance and 330 pF give a 78 V spike per ampere, 6.2 MHz ringing and a 78 Ω characteristic impedance; with 1000 pF added the ringing falls to 3.1 MHz. Power "p = 2f(1/2C V²) = f C V²": 1000 pF at 400 V and 100 kHz dissipates 16.0 W.
- **J. Falin, TI SLVA255, Sep 2006, "Minimizing Ringing at the Switch Node of a Boost Converter"** [Established]: ringing at 217 MHz, halved to 113 MHz by 300 pF, so the parasitic capacitance is 100 pF (the paper's C_ADD/3 approximation) and the inductance 5.4 nH; the snubber resistor is the characteristic impedance (7.3 Ω, rounded to 10 Ω) with 330 pF; the peak fell from 9.8 V to 8 V. The note counts the snubber loss as ½·C·V²·f.
- **[Contested] terminology and loss formula (our analysis):** both notes call $R = \sqrt{L/C}$ "critical damping", but with $C_d$ = 3–4 C the ringing pair has ζ ≈ 0.5 (chapter 07-03, Example 07-03.5). The loss is $C_dV^2f$ when the snubber capacitor is charged from a voltage source through resistance (Todd) and approaches ½C_dV²f when an inductor's current charges it (SLVA255's boost turn-off).
- **L. Balogh, TI SLUA618A, "Fundamentals of MOSFET and IGBT Gate Driver Circuits", Mar 2017, rev. Oct 2018** [Established]: "the source inductor and the CISS capacitor form a resonant circuit"; "RGATE,OPT = 2 × √(LS/CISS) − (RDRV + RG,I)" (Eq. 15), the series-RLC critical-damping rule, with $L_S$ the inductance that resonates with $C_{ISS}$ in the gate-drive loop (the course audit's web search found TI's note SLLA385 estimating it from the ringing frequency; that note was not opened here, and ti.com was unreachable when re-checked); for transformer coupling, "RC ≥ 2 × √(LM/CC)". The note's sentence that higher resistance will "underdamp" the oscillation is evidently a slip for overdamp, and is not quoted.
- **Sources:**
  - [Todd, SEM-900 Topic 2 (TI E2E copy)](https://e2e.ti.com/cfs-file/__key/communityserver-discussions-components-files/196/SEM_5F00_900.pdf).
  - [TI SLVA255](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/slva255/slva255.pdf).
  - [TI SLUA618A](https://www.ti.com/lit/ml/slua618a/slua618a.pdf).
  - Checked Nov 2026.

## R-114 · Oscilloscope probe ground-lead ringing

*Gathered by a research subagent (07-03 facts file, entries F-0744 to F-0746).*

- **Tektronix, *ABCs of Probes* primer, 60W-6053-17, Jun 2024** [Established for the mechanism; the 140 MHz figure is a single-vendor typical value]: "The ground lead reactive elements L and Cin form a series resonant circuit with only Rin for damping. When this series resonant circuit is hit with a pulse, it will ring"; "an 11 pF passive probe with 6-inch ground lead will ring at about 140 MHz"; with a 100 MHz scope the ringing "may not be seen at all", with a 200 MHz scope it "will be apparent"; "If you see the ringing frequency change … you'll know that it's ground-lead related"; ground springs for signals below 30 Vrms. The primer gives no inductance value.
- **Tektronix P6136 instruction manual, 070-6025-04 (undated; distributor mirror)** [Established for this probe; Industry estimate as a general value]: "A six-inch ground lead has approximately 150 nH of inductance, which produces an f0 of approximately 130 MHz" with the probe's typical 10.8 pF; the low-inductance lead gives "approximately 32 nH instead of 150 nH". Our arithmetic gives 125 MHz.
- **Teledyne LeCroy, "Passive Probe Ground Lead Effects", Jun 2013** [Industry estimate]: "Using 20 nH per inch as a rule of thumb"; a 10-inch ground loop of about 200 nH with a 9.5 pF probe cut the −3 dB bandwidth "from >500 MHz to 175 MHz" with resonance at 115 MHz; ground blade or spring accessories "may have an inductance of 10-20 nH".
- **Keysight, "Understanding Oscilloscope Probe Specifications", 3124-1574.EN, Aug 2024** [Industry estimate]: probe-extension wires add "as much as 25 nH of inductive loading per inch", and ground leads similarly add uncompensated inductance.
- **Sources:**
  - [Tektronix ABCs of Probes](https://download.tek.com/document/02_ABCs-of-Probes-Primer.pdf).
  - [Tektronix P6136 manual (mirror)](https://valuetronics.com/cdn/shop/files/Tektronix_P6136_Instruction_Manual.pdf).
  - [Teledyne LeCroy note](https://www.teledynelecroy.com/doc/passive-probe-ground-lead-effects).
  - [Keysight app note (distributor mirror)](https://assets.testequity.com/te1/Documents/pdf/keysight/Keysight_Understanding-Oscilloscope-Probe-Specifications_App_Note_1124.pdf).
  - Checked Nov 2026.

## R-115 · GaN power-loop inductance, switching speed and output capacitance

*Gathered by a research subagent (07-03 facts file, entries F-0747 to F-0749).*

- **EPC white papers WP010 ("Optimizing PCB Layout", 2019) and WP009 ("Impact of Parasitics on Performance", 2020), D. Reusch** [Industry estimate for the figures, one vendor's boards; Established for the mechanism]: in a 12 V to 1.2 V, 1 MHz eGaN buck, switch-node overshoot fell "from 100% of the input voltage to 30%" when the high-frequency loop inductance fell from 1.6 nH to 0.4 nH; raising it "from around 0.4 nH to 2.9 nH results in additional loss, decreasing efficiency by over 4%"; eGaN package inductance is "a couple hundred pico Henry", so the PCB layout dominates. A 2021 EPC webinar shows 70% overshoot at about 1.0 nH and 30% at about 0.4 nH (20 A).
- **EPC How2AppNote 007 (2021)** [Industry estimate]: a 48 V to 12 V, 500 kHz buck with EPC2045 reached 60 V/ns against 12 V/ns for a 100 V silicon MOSFET at the same peak overshoot; "Parasitic inductances cause higher overshoot voltages and slower switching transitions."
- **TI LMG3422R030/LMG3426R030/LMG3427R030 datasheet, SNOSDA7F, rev. Aug 2024** [Established]: "20V/ns to 150V/ns slew rate"; power-loop inductance estimated from the ringing frequency, "Lpl = 1/(4π² f²ring Cring)", with Cring the output capacitance at the bus voltage plus board and load capacitance; "Typically, the power loop inductance of the Layout Example is around 2.5nH"; COSS 170 pF typical (130 pF minimum) at 400 V; time-related output capacitance 430 pF (0–400 V).
- **EPC2045 datasheet, revised 4 Feb 2025** [Established]: COSS 295 pF typical, 443 pF maximum at 50 V; COSS(TR) 500 pF (0–50 V); CISS 767 pF typical; internal gate resistance 0.6 Ω.
- **Caveat:** output capacitance depends strongly on voltage; state which value (at the operating voltage, or time-related) a ringing estimate uses. Our arithmetic: 2.5 nH with 170 pF rings at about 244 MHz.
- **Sources:**
  - [EPC WP010](https://epc-co.com/epc/documents/papers/Optimizing%20PCB%20Layout%20with%20eGaN%20FETs.pdf).
  - [EPC WP009](https://epc-co.com/epc/Portals/0/epc/documents/papers/Impact%20of%20Parasitcs%20on%20Performance.pdf).
  - [EPC layout webinar](https://epc-co.com/epc/Portals/0/epc/documents/presentations/High-Performance%20Layout%20Techniques%20to%20Maximize%20GaN%20Device%20Performance.pdf).
  - [EPC How2AppNote 007](https://epc-co.com/epc/Portals/0/epc/documents/application-notes/How2AppNote007%20-%20Design%20an%20eGaN%20FET%20Based%20Power%20Stage%20with%20an%20Optimal%20Layout.pdf).
  - [EPC2045 datasheet](https://epc-co.com/epc/Portals/0/epc/documents/datasheets/EPC2045_datasheet.pdf).
  - [TI LMG342xR030 datasheet](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/lmg3422r030.pdf).
  - Checked Nov 2026.

## R-116 · PDN target impedance in vendor practice

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entry F-0715).* Extends R-032.

- **TI, "Sitara Processor Power Distribution Networks: Implementation and Analysis", SPRAC76H (Nov 2022, revised Oct 2025), §5 and Table 7-1** [Established]: "ZTARGET = (Voltage Rail × %Ripple)/(0.5 × IMax)" (the 0.5 assumes a step of half the maximum current); the impedance must stay below target from DC to F_MAX, beyond which "the dominance of the parasitic planar spreading inductance and package inductances" prevents further improvement; "Typical mid-frequency capacitors have capacitance in the range of 1 nF –100 nF, ESR in the range of 10-100 mΩ, and ESL in the range of 0.5 nH – 1 nH"; for the AM570x VDD_CORE rail, a 57 mΩ target up to 20 MHz with a decoupling-loop inductance of at most 2 nH. The regulator is modeled as a series R–L that turns inductive above about 0.5–1 MHz. No package-inductance value is given.
- **Source:** [TI SPRAC76H](https://www.ti.com/document-viewer/lit/html/SPRAC76G/GUID-AF67D124-C8FB-4892-A7E1-D65C51297D92). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-117 · LDO stability and output-capacitor ESR

*Gathered by a research subagent (07-04 facts file, entries F-0751 to F-0758); every document below was opened.*

- **J. Falin and J. Cummings, TI SLVA115A, "ESR, Stability, and the LDO Regulator" (May 2002, rev. Feb 2020)** [Established]: "there are three important poles in a PMOS or PNP pass element based LDO regulator" (dominant, load, pass device); using the output capacitor's ESR to create a zero is "typically the simplest and least expensive method"; the ESR must be high enough for a −20 dB/decade slope at crossover and low enough for the gain to fall below 0 dB before the pass-device pole; for TPS76050 with 2.2 µF, "the ESR must be between 0.1 Ω and 20 Ω"; "Typically, four rings or less indicate sufficient phase margin" [Industry estimate]. **Error in the note:** Equation 1 prints $f_{Z(ESR)}$ = 72.3 kHz for 0.1 Ω and 2.2 µF; the correct value is 723 kHz. Its Figure 2 also labels the ESR axis in µF.
- **TI TPS760xx datasheet, SLVS144D (Jul 1998, rev. May 2001)** [Established]: "The minimum recommended capacitance value is 2.2 µF and the ESR … must be between 0.1 Ω and 20 Ω. Capacitor values of 2.5-µF or larger are acceptable, provided the ESR is less than 20 Ω."
- **B. S. Lee, TI SLVA072, "Technical Review of Low Dropout Voltage Regulator Operation and Performance" (Aug 1999)** [Established]: the stable-ESR plot "is called tunnel of death. The curve shows that the ESR must be between 0.2 Ω and 9 Ω" (an unnamed typical LDO with 4.7 µF).
- **TI AN-1482, SNVA167A, "LDO Regulator Stability Using Ceramic Output Capacitors" (May 2006, rev. Apr 2013)** [Established for the mechanism; Industry estimate for typical values]: "a typical 10 µF Tantalum capacitor might have an ESR in the range of about 0.5 Ω, giving a zero at a frequency of about 30 kHz"; "A 10 µF capacitor whose ESR is in the 5 mΩ range is providing a zero at a frequency above 3 MHz"; the ESR zero raised an illustrative loop's phase margin "from 11° up to about 68°"; electrolytic-stable LDOs about 0.1–10 Ω, ceramic-stable versions with an internal zero from 0 Ω up to about 0.5 Ω.
- **T. Schiff, onsemi AND8037/D, "Stability in High Speed Linear LDO Regulators" (Oct 2000)** [Established]: older devices specified "a minimum ESR for the output capacitor … with no limitations on the maximum"; design guidelines come from keeping the poles in the left half-plane; "making the ESR larger or smaller tends to make the response more unstable" (for its LDO-controller example).
- **Current parts** [Established]: TI TPS7A26 (SBVS290B, rev. Oct 2019) and TPS7A25 (SBVS372C, rev. Dec 2022): "Stable with 1-µF output capacitors", C_OUT 1 µF min, 2.2 µF nom, 100 µF max, "All capacitor values are assumed to derate to 50% of the nominal capacitor value"; TPS7A20 (SBVS338H, rev. Jul 2024): "Stable with 1µF minimum ceramic output capacitor", ESR 100 mΩ maximum, effective capacitance 0.47–200 µF; onsemi NCP114 (Rev. 31, Jan 2024): stable with 0.22 µF minimum effective capacitance, "no requirement for the minimum value of Equivalent Series Resistance … but the maximum value of ESR should be less than 2 Ω", tantalum not recommended.
- **Sources:** [SLVA115A](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/slva115a/slva115a.pdf); [TPS760](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/tps760.pdf); [SLVA072](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/slva072/slva072.pdf); [SNVA167A](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/snva167a/snva167a.pdf); [AND8037](https://www.onsemi.com/pub/Collateral/AND8037-D.PDF); [TPS7A26](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/tps7a26.pdf); [TPS7A25](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/tps7a25.pdf); [TPS7A20](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/tps7a20.pdf); [NCP114](https://www.onsemi.com/download/data-sheet/pdf/ncp114-d.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-118 · Pole-zero analysis and s-domain models in ngspice

*Gathered by a research subagent (07-04 facts file, entries F-0759 to F-0762).*

- **H. Vogt, G. Atkinson, D. Warning, P. Nenzi, *Ngspice User's Manual*, version 47 (11 Aug 2026)**, opened from the SourceForge release folder (the ngspice documentation page itself returned 403) [Established]:
  - `.pz` (§1.2.4, §11.3.6): computes poles and/or zeros of a voltage-ratio (`vol`) or transimpedance (`cur`) transfer function; "The method used in the analysis is a sub-optimal numerical search. For large circuits it may take a considerable time or fail to find all poles and zeros. … for some circuits, the method becomes 'lost' and may find an excessive number of poles or zeros."
  - `s_xfer` (§8.2.18): an XSPICE "s-domain transfer function" model for AC and transient analysis; the numerator degree may not exceed the denominator's; frequencies are in radians per second; it is the documented substitute for a LAPLACE-type source (§5.2.5).
- **Version note:** this repository and its CI run ngspice-42; the lab's `.pz` netlist was checked on version 42 (roots printed in rad/s).
- **Source:** [ngspice manuals](https://sourceforge.net/projects/ngspice/files/ng-spice-rework/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-119 · Loop-gain measurement by injection

*Gathered by a research subagent (07-04 facts file, entries F-0763 to F-0766).*

- **R. D. Middlebrook, "Measurement of loop gain in feedback systems," *International Journal of Electronics* 38(4):485–512, April 1975, DOI 10.1080/00207217508920421** [Established, from the abstract; full text not opened]: "In high loop-gain systems it is desirable that the loop-gain measurement be made without opening the loop"; the paper treats voltage- and current-injection techniques, measurement of unstable systems, and combinations of both.
- **F. Hämmerle, OMICRON Lab Information Note "Loop Gain Measurement: The Voltage Injection Method …", V1.1 (2017)** [Established]: an injection resistor of "10 Ω is generally suitable for voltage regulators and DC/DC converters"; the measured $T_v = T(1 + Z_{out}/Z_{in}) + Z_{out}/Z_{in}$, valid when $\lvert Z_{in}\rvert \gg \lvert Z_{out}\rvert$ and $\lvert T\rvert \gg \lvert Z_{out}/Z_{in}\rvert$; the injection level should be small. OMICRON's DC/DC application note (V3.3, 2018) reads phase margin against 0° in its closed-loop setup and shows PM = 83.1°, GM = 9.2 dB; the Bode 100 R2 covers 1 Hz–50 MHz (technical data V1.6, 2026).
- **TI AN-1889, SNVA364A, "How to Measure the Loop Transfer Function of Power Supplies" (Oct 2008, rev. Apr 2013)** [Established; the margin is a rule of thumb]: measurement with a signal generator and an oscilloscope; "A good value is a 20Ω resistor"; "a minimum phase margin of 45 to 50 degrees is necessary. More is better."
- **Sources:** [Middlebrook (DOI)](https://doi.org/10.1080/00207217508920421); [OMICRON information note](https://www.omicron-lab.com/fileadmin/assets/Bode_100/InformationNotes/Bode_Info_LoopGain_V1_1.pdf); [TI SNVA364A](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/snva364a/snva364a.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-120 · Decompensated op-amps

*Gathered by a research subagent (07-04 facts file, entries F-0767 and F-0768).*

- **TI OPA627/OPA637 datasheet, SBOS165C (Aug 2000, rev. Jan 2025)** [Established]: "The OPA627 is unity-gain stable. The OPA637 is stable in gains ≥ 5"; circuits with a feedback capacitor "must use the OPA627 for proper stability"; the OPA637's gain–bandwidth is 80 MHz at G = 10. The OPA627's gain–bandwidth differs by grade in the datasheet tables (16 MHz or 45 MHz) [Contested by grade; not used].
- **TI OPA847 (SBOS251E, rev. Dec 2008) and OPA842 (SBOS267D, rev. Sep 2010)** [Established]: the OPA847 "is stable down to gains as low as +12V/V", with a 3.9 GHz typical gain–bandwidth product; the OPA842 is unity-gain stable with a 200 MHz gain–bandwidth product.
- **Sources:** [OPA627/637](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/opa627.pdf); [OPA847](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/opa847.pdf); [OPA842](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/opa842.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-121 · Heaviside's operational calculus and network synthesis

*Gathered by a research subagent (07-04 facts file, entries F-0769 to F-0774); network-synthesis references checked on Crossref.*

- **Heaviside** (MacTutor history archive, St Andrews; ETHW, IEEE History Center) [Established for the story; dates as stated by MacTutor]: born 18 May 1850, died 3 February 1925 (ETHW gives other dates for both; [Contested]); "His operational calculus, developed between 1880 and 1887, caused much controversy"; he replaced $d/dx$ by a variable $p$; "the correctness of Heaviside's calculus was not proved until Bromwich's work"; a paper submitted to the Royal Society was rejected. His "On operators in physical mathematics", *Proc. R. Soc. Lond.* 52:504–529 (1893) and 54:105–143 (1894), known from metadata only.
- **T. J. I'A. Bromwich, "Normal coordinates in dynamical systems," *Proc. London Math. Soc.* (2) 15:401–448** (received 22 April 1916; volume year 1917), DOI 10.1112/plms/s2-15.1.401, scan opened [Established]: solves dynamical problems "by means of complex integrals" and proves that Heaviside's "method of resistance-operators" is equivalent.
- **J. R. Carson, "The Heaviside operational calculus," *Bell System Technical Journal* 1(2):43–55, Nov 1922** (abstract only) and ***Electric Circuit Theory and the Operational Calculus*, McGraw-Hill, 1926** (scan opened) [Established]: the operational formula is a shorthand for an integral equation "of the Laplace type".
- **Network synthesis** (Crossref metadata) [Established]: O. Brune, "Synthesis of a finite two-terminal network whose driving-point impedance is a prescribed function of frequency," *Journal of Mathematics and Physics* 10:191–236 (1931), DOI 10.1002/sapm1931101191; R. Bott and R. J. Duffin, "Impedance synthesis without use of transformers," *Journal of Applied Physics* 20:816 (1949), DOI 10.1063/1.1698532.
- **Sources:** [MacTutor](https://mathshistory.st-andrews.ac.uk/Biographies/Heaviside/); [ETHW](https://ethw.org/Oliver_Heaviside); [Bromwich (DOI)](https://doi.org/10.1112/plms/s2-15.1.401); [Carson 1922 (DOI)](https://doi.org/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1922.tb00388.x); [Brune (DOI)](https://doi.org/10.1002/sapm1931101191); [Bott and Duffin (DOI)](https://doi.org/10.1063/1.1698532). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-122 · Power factor, harmonics and utility tariffs

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0716 to F-0721); documents opened unless stated.*

- **IEC 61000-3-2:2018, edition 5.0** (preview PDF hosted by VDE Verlag; IEC Webstore) [Established for scope and status]: "applicable to electrical and electronic equipment having a rated input current up to and including 16 A per phase, and intended to be connected to public low-voltage distribution systems"; "For systems with nominal voltages less than but not equal to 220 V (line-to-neutral), the limits have not yet been considered"; the Webstore lists the consolidated "IEC 61000-3-2:2018+AMD1:2020+AMD2:2024". The Class D clause text was not in the preview.
- **onsemi, *Power Factor Correction (PFC) Handbook*, HBD853/D Rev. 5 (Apr 2014), ch. 1** [Industry estimate: manufacturer handbook; the standard's clause not read]: IEC 61000-3-2 "applies to most electrical appliances with input power of 75 W (Class D equipment) or greater, and it specifies the maximum amplitude of line-frequency harmonics up to and including the 39th harmonic"; Class D covers "personal computers, televisions and monitors". Per-watt Class D limits seen only in secondary sources are not used.
- **80 PLUS program (CLEAResult pages, read 19 Nov 2026)** [Established]: for 115 V internal non-redundant supplies the summary table gives PF ≥ 0.90 for Standard, Bronze, Silver and Gold and PF ≥ 0.95 for Platinum, Titanium and Ruby, with Titanium (90/92/94/90% at 10/20/50/100% load) and Ruby (85/91/93/95/91% at 5/10/20/50/100%) holding PF ≥ 0.95 at 20% load. For redundant data-center supplies (230/277/480 V, 380/800 V DC), Ruby: 90/91/95/96.5/92% at 5/10/20/50/100% load with PF ≥ 0.96 from 20% load; Titanium: 90/94/96/91% at 10/20/50/100% with PF ≥ 0.95 at 20%. **[Contested]:** the page's per-tier text gives PF ≥ 0.95 for Bronze to Gold, against 0.90 in its table; the chapter follows the table.
- **EPRI and CLEAResult, *Generalized Test Protocol for Calculating the Energy Efficiency of Internal Ac-Dc and Dc-Dc Power Supplies*, Rev. 6.7.3 (22 Jul 2025), §3.12–3.13** [Established]: "True power factor is the ratio of the active, or real, power (P) consumed in watts to the apparent power (S) drawn in volt-amperes"; "This definition of power factor includes the effect of both displacement and distortion"; "For a pure sinusoidal waveshape the crest factor is 1.414". (The protocol's $S = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2}$ holds only for sinusoids; our note.)
- **Utility power-factor adjustments (customer pages, read 19 Nov 2026)** [Established; thresholds are utility-specific and change]: Austin Energy, "Customers with a power factor less than 90% will see an adjustment to the kilowatt demand", example 13.5 kW at PF 0.87 → 14.0 kW billed; Minnesota Power, "Billed kW = 100 kW x 0.90 power factor / 0.70 power factor = 129 kW"; City of Dover, Delaware, measured demand × 0.95 / measured PF, example 250 kW at PF 0.85 → 279.4 kW.
- **Sources:** [IEC 61000-3-2 preview](https://www.vde-verlag.de/iec-normen/preview-pdf/info_iec61000-3-2%7Bed5.0%7Db.pdf); [IEC Webstore](https://webstore.iec.ch/en/publication/28164); [onsemi HBD853/D](https://www.onsemi.com/pub/collateral/hbd853-d.pdf); [80 PLUS ratings](https://www.clearesult.com/80plus/80plus-psu-ratings-explained); [80 PLUS test protocol](https://www.clearesult.com/80plus/sites/80plus/files/2025-08/Generalized%2520Test%2520Protocol_Rev%25206.7.3%25201.pdf); [Austin Energy](https://austinenergy.com/rates/commercial-rates/power-factor-adjustment); [Minnesota Power](https://mnpower.com/CustomerService/PowerFactorBilling); [City of Dover](https://www.cityofdover.gov/media/Directory/Customer%20Services/Utility/News%20and%20Info/CommercialPDF2.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-123 · Impedance-measurement instruments

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0775 to F-0777); documents opened unless stated.*

- **Rohde & Schwarz, *R&S LCX LCR Meter, Data Sheet*, PD 3609.8309.32, v04.00 (Dec 2025)** [Established]: "The R&S®LCX100 LCR meter covers the frequency range from 4 Hz to 300 kHz. The R&S®LCX200 has an upper frequency limit of 500 kHz and can be extended to 1 MHz or 10 MHz using software options"; "The basic accuracy for impedance measurements is ±0.05 %, and for phase measurements ±0.03°"; measurement range 10 mΩ to 100 MΩ; "Phase measurement accuracy in deg (°) = (180/π) × impedance (measurement accuracy in %/100)"; "BA in % = accuracy in % + (Zm/Zo × 100) + (Zs/Zm × 100)", with LCX200 residuals Zo/Zs of 2 GΩ/1 mΩ (≤ 1 kHz), 1 GΩ/1 mΩ (1–10 kHz), 250 MΩ/1.5 mΩ (10–100 kHz), 150 MΩ/1.5 mΩ (0.1–1 MHz), 10 MΩ/10 mΩ (1–5 MHz), 1 MΩ/30 mΩ (5–10 MHz); examples "1 kΩ at 1 MHz ▷ 0.2 % basic accuracy or 1 kΩ at 100 Hz ▷ 0.1 %", "100 kΩ at 1 kHz ▷ 0.1 % … or 3 Ω at 1 kHz ▷ 0.2 %"; valid "only … with prior open/short correction", and further multiplied by coefficients for signal level, speed, cable, bias, temperature and frequency.
- **HIOKI, *Impedance Analyzer IM3570* datasheet IM3570E14-23M ("correct as of Mar. 11, 2022"; distributor-hosted copy)** [Established]: "measurement frequencies of 4 Hz to 5 MHz"; "Basic accuracy Z : ±0.08%rdg. θ: ±0.05°"; test signal 5 mV–5 V rms to 1 MHz, 10 mV–1 V rms above; "Measurement accuracy = Basic accuracy × C × D × E × F × G". Hioki's own product page returned 403 and was not opened.
- **Zurich Instruments, MFIA web page (read 19 Nov 2026) and *Product Specification: MFIA* (Feb 2023)** [Established for frequency range and core accuracy]: 1 mHz to 500 kHz, "extended to 5 MHz when upgraded"; "a 0.05% accuracy is specified between 1 mHz and 500 kHz, and 1 Ω and 1 MΩ"; "Accuracy valid if parameter is the dominant value of the circuit representation". **[Contested]:** the reduced-accuracy range is "10 mΩ to 1 GΩ" on the web page and "20 mΩ to 50 GΩ" in the leaflet. Web-page price "CHF 12,710 … ex-works Zurich and only valid for the selected region", variant not stated [Industry estimate].
- **Not opened:** Keysight E4980A/E4990A documents (a PDF request redirected to HTML).
- **Sources:** [R&S LCX data sheet](https://scdn.rohde-schwarz.com/ur/pws/dl_downloads/pdm/cl_brochures_and_datasheets/data_sheet/3609_8309_32/LCX_dat_en_3609-8309-32_v0400.pdf); [Hioki IM3570 datasheet (Meilhaus copy)](https://www.meilhaus.de/cosmoshop/default/articleMedia/hioki-im3570/de/Datasheet_HIOKI_IM3570_en.pdf); [Zurich Instruments MFIA](https://www.zhinst.com/en/products/mfia-impedance-analyzer); [MFIA leaflet](https://www.zhinst.com/sites/default/files/documents/2024-10/zi_mfia_leaflet.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-124 · Bioimpedance in wearables

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0778 to F-0782); documents opened unless stated.*

- **Texas Instruments, A. Udupa, *Body Composition Measurement (BCM) for Wearables*, application brief SBAA562 (Nov 2022)** [Established]: "The basis of a BIA device is the injection of an excitation current into the body at one or more frequencies using a pair of electrodes, and sensing of voltage using another pair of electrodes. Single-frequency (SF) BIA uses a single frequency, most commonly 50 kHz. Multiple-frequency (MF) BIA uses multiple frequencies (for example, 5 kHz to 250 kHz). The biggest challenge of accurate BIA on a wearable device is the high and time-varying contact impedances"; "A sine-wave excitation at a programmable frequency is generated using a Direct Digital Synthesis (DDS) engine. The received signal is I-Q demodulated in analog to recover the amplitude and phase of the impedance"; requirement table: 4 electrodes, excitation about 50 kHz, body impedance about 1 kΩ, accuracy < 2% (AFE4500: 5–250 kHz, 100 Ω–3 kΩ, 1% "in the presence of several kΩ of contact impedance"). TI product overview SBAT016 (Oct 2023): body impedance measured "usually at 50 kHz". The AFE4500 datasheet and product page returned HTTP 404 on 19 Nov 2026; the part's status is unknown.
- **Analog Devices, *MAX30009* datasheet 19-101213 Rev 1 (Feb 2022)** [Industry estimate: read only through a third-party rendering of the datasheet text; analog.com could not be reached]: "sine-wave current generator … 16Hz to 806kHz and … 16nA to 1.28mA[RMS]"; "BIA/BIS Frequencies from 1kHz to 806kHz"; bipolar and tetrapolar configurations. **[Contested]** in secondary sources (search excerpts quote 891 kHz and 500 kHz upper limits).
- **Samsung support pages** (South Africa, "Last Update date : Aug 16. 2021"; US answer ANS10003647, read 19 Nov 2026) [Established as Samsung's statements]: the BIA sensor "sends microcurrents to the body"; a reading takes about 15 seconds with two fingers on the side keys; results achieve "98% correlation to Dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry scans". No excitation frequency or electrode count is stated on these pages; a four-electrode layout appears only in a search excerpt of a Samsung Newsroom article that could not be opened.
- **Samsung Mobile Press, "Samsung and Massachusetts General Hospital Launch Joint Study To Investigate GLP-1 Treatment Monitoring With Galaxy Watch" (28 May 2026)** [Established]: the BioActive Sensor "integrates three sensors into a single chipset: an optical heart rate sensor (PPG), an electrical heart rate sensor (ECG) and a bioelectrical impedance analysis sensor (BIA)", in Galaxy Watch devices "since the release of Galaxy Watch4 in 2021"; the study enrolls 100 adults and uses DXA as the reference.
- **J. P. Bennett, Y. E. Liu, N. N. Kelly, B. K. Quon, M. C. Wong, C. McCarthy, S. B. Heymsfield, J. A. Shepherd, "Next-generation smart watches to estimate whole-body composition using bioimpedance analysis: accuracy and precision in a diverse, multiethnic sample," *Am. J. Clin. Nutr.* 116(5):1418–1429 (Nov 2022), doi:10.1093/ajcn/nqac200** [Established; abstract only, full text not reachable]: "Of 109 participants recruited, 75 subjects completed the full manufacturer-recommended protocol"; "Significant fat-free mass (FFM) differences (P < 0.05) were observed between both W-BIA and 8-BIA when compared to DXA, though the systematic biases to the criterion were correctable"; "Lin's concordance correlation coefficient = 0.97" between watch and laboratory BIA; FFM precision "CV, 0.7% … versus 1.3% … for W-BIA".
- **Sources:** [TI SBAA562](https://www.ti.com/lit/pdf/sbaa562); [TI SBAT016](https://www.ti.com/lit/pdf/sbat016); [Samsung ZA support](https://www.samsung.com/za/support/mobile-devices/measure-your-body-composition-with-the-galaxy-watch4-series/); [Samsung US support](https://www.samsung.com/us/support/answer/ANS10003647/); [Samsung Mobile Press](https://samsungmobilepress.com/articles/samsung-massachusetts-general-hospital-glp-1-monitoring); [Bennett et al. 2022](https://doi.org/10.1093/ajcn/nqac200). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-125 · Battery impedance spectroscopy in BMS ICs

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0783 and F-0784); documents opened unless stated.*

- **Texas Instruments** [Established; BQ79881-Q1 figures are preproduction]:
  - *BQ79826Z-Q1 Automotive 26S Battery Monitor with Smart EIS Engine*, SLVSJC3A (Sep 2025, revised Sep 2026; production data): "Impedance Accuracy: 1% (with 1A excitation and 200uΩ impedance) – Measurement frequency: 0.01Hz to 3.5kHz – Precise device-to-device I/V synchronization".
  - *BQ79881-Q1*, SLVSJV2 (Sep 2025; advance information): "Excitation frequency: 0.01Hz – 3.5kHz – < 5us I/V synchronization from device to device".
  - B. Burk, *Next-Generation Battery Monitoring With Electrochemical Impedance Spectroscopy*, white paper STDA020A (Jun 2026): the engine "reduces measurement time by exciting up to five impedance frequencies simultaneously"; a single impedance at 500 Hz "moves as the temperature changes", used to "identify the core temperature of the battery".
  - W. Tai, Y. Wu, application note SPVA053 (Apr 2026): Nyquist plots for 314 Ah cells from 0.1 Hz to 2 kHz, with magnitude deviation "< 2%" and phase deviation "< 1°".
- **NXP** [Established for architecture and dates]:
  - Press release, Eindhoven, 29 Oct 2025: an "Electrochemical Impedance Spectroscopy (EIS) battery management chipset with hardware-based nanosecond-level synchronization of all devices": BMA7418 cell sensing device, BMA6402 gateway and BMA8420 battery junction box controller, "with directly in-chip integrated discrete Fourier transformation".
  - *BMA7418SB; BMA7418TB* short data sheet Rev 1.0 (24 Jun 2026): "Integrated single frequency discrete fourier transform (DFT) for EIS support – Extraction of one selectable frequency".
  - White paper *Electrochemical Impedance Spectroscopy – Moves out of the Lab and into the Field* (2025): "The frequency range in EIS typically spans from multiple kHz to mHz. With the 1kHz measurement being a long-standing industry standard in short circuit detection in manufacturing and 1-10Hz as the usual control frequency of classic BMS. Higher frequencies probe inductive and ohmic behaviors, while low frequencies reveal diffusion and capacitive effects. However, measuring at very low frequencies (<0.1Hz) requires long acquisition times, increasing the risk of drift and noise."
  - NXP's BMS-EIS solution page gives a range of "0.1 - 1 kHz", which is ambiguous (0.1 Hz or 0.1 kHz at the low end) and is not used.
- **Derived (ours, not vendor statements):** 1 A × 200 µΩ = 200 µV; a sampling skew Δt is a phase error of 360° f Δt.
- **Sources:** [BQ79826Z-Q1](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/bq79826z-q1.pdf); [BQ79881-Q1](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/bq79881-q1.pdf); [STDA020A](https://www.ti.com/lit/pdf/stda020); [SPVA053](https://www.ti.com/lit/pdf/spva053); [NXP press release](https://www.nxp.com/company/about-nxp/newsroom/NW-NXP-IMPROVES-BATTERY-HEALTH-EIS-CHIPSET); [BMA7418 short data sheet](https://www.nxp.com/docs/en/data-sheet/BMA7418SB_BMA7418TB_SDS.pdf); [NXP EIS white paper](https://www.nxp.com/docs/en/white-paper/EIS-NXP-WP.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-126 · SPICE AC analysis

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entry F-0785); document opened.*

- **H. Vogt, G. Atkinson, D. Warning, P. Nenzi, *Ngspice User's Manual*, version 47 (11 Aug 2026)** [Established]: §1.2.2, "AC analysis … represents the small signal, sinusoidal solution of the analog system"; "The program first computes the dc operating point of the circuit and determines linearized, small-signal models for all of the nonlinear devices"; "If the circuit has only one ac input, it is convenient to set that input to unity and zero phase, so that output variables have the same value as the transfer function". §11.3.1: ".ac dec nd fstart fstop / .ac oct no fstart fstop / .ac lin np fstart fstop"; "All L and C devices get their imaginary value that depends on the actual frequency step … The resulting node voltages (and branch currents) are complex vectors"; with more than one AC source "the result will be a superposition of all sources and difficult to interpret". Vector functions: "ph(vector) Phase of complex vector, in radians"; "db(vector) 20 log10(mag(vector))".
- The repository's CI runs ngspice 42; a local check of an RC low-pass at its corner returned magnitude 0.7071, −3.0103 dB and −45°, consistent with the v47 description.
- **Source:** [Ngspice manual v47](https://downloads.sourceforge.net/project/ngspice/ng-spice-rework/47/ngspice-47-manual.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-127 · Bode's gain–phase work

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0786 and F-0787); documents opened.*

- **H. W. Bode, "Relations Between Attenuation and Phase in Feedback Amplifier Design," *Bell System Technical Journal* 19(3):421–454, July 1940, doi:10.1002/j.1538-7305.1940.tb00839.x** [Established; metadata from Crossref, text from the Internet Archive's OCR of the scan, with OCR errors corrected in brackets]: "unless he can finally adjust the phase and attenuation characteristics around the feedback loop so the amplifier will not spontaneously burst into uncontrollable singing, none of these advantages can actually be realized"; "the price in terms of cutoff interval is roughly one octave for each ten db of feedback in the useful band. In practice, an additional allowance of an octave or so … must be made"; "in an amplifier with 30 db feedback, the frequency interval over which effective control of the loop transmission characteristics is necessary is at least four octaves, or sixteen times, broader than the useful band. If we raise the feedback to 60 db, the effective range must be more than a hundred times the useful range"; the phase-area theorem stated with attenuation in nepers and phase in radians; "an attenuation having a constant slope of 6[k] db per octave … is equal numerically to [kπ/2] radians"; the limiting phase "taken as 180° less some definite margin", the phase margin. Re-check the bracketed OCR corrections against the page images before quoting them in print.
- **H. W. Bode, *Network Analysis and Feedback Amplifier Design*, D. Van Nostrand Company, 1945** (Bell Telephone Laboratories Series; xii + 551 pp.; LCCN 45009525; no DOI) [Established, from the eleventh printing's front matter and library records]: "First Published September 1945"; preface: "The bulk of the material was prepared in 1938 and 1939 and was given in course form to my colleagues there in the winters of 1939-40 and 1940-41"; signed "April 1945".
- **Sources:** [Crossref record](https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1940.tb00839.x); [Internet Archive, BSTJ 19(3)](https://archive.org/details/bstj19-3-421); [Internet Archive, the 1945 book (11th printing)](https://archive.org/details/dli.ernet.15701); [Open Library](https://openlibrary.org/works/OL7688069W). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-128 · Decibel and neper standards

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0788 to F-0790); documents opened unless stated.*

- **A. Thompson, B. N. Taylor, *Guide for the Use of the International System of Units (SI)*, NIST SP 811, 2008 edition, §8.7** [Established]: "Level-of-a-field-quantity is defined by the relation LF = ln(F/F0) … Level-of-a-power-quantity is defined by the relation LP = (1/2) ln(P/P0) … (Note that if P/P0 = (F/F0)², then LP = LF.)"; "LF = 2 lg(F/F0) B = 20 lg(F/F0) dB"; "LP = lg(P/P0) B = 10 lg(P/P0) dB"; "1 B = (ln 10)/2 Np exactly ≈ 1.151 293 Np; 1 dB ≈ 0.115 129 3 Np"; "When reporting values of LF and LP, one must always give the reference level." Its line for a power-level difference, "ΔLP = … = ln(P1/P2)", omits the ½ that its own definition requires; not reproduced.
- **BIPM, *The International System of Units (SI)*, 9th edition 2019, V4.01 (June 2026), Table 8 note (m)** [Established]: the neper, bel and decibel "are used to express values of specified logarithmic ratio quantities. When using these units, it is important that the quantity be specified, and that any reference value used be specified"; the neper is "usually applied to logarithmic amplitude ratios" and the decibel to "logarithmic power ratios". **NIST SP 330 (2019):** "accepted by the CIPM for use with the International System, but are not SI units".
- **IEC 60027-3:2002, edition 3.0** [Established for edition and scope; preview and webstore only]: "logarithmic ratios which are defined by the logarithm of the ratio of two field quantities of the same kind or of two power quantities of the same kind"; the IEC Webstore page (read 19 Nov 2026) shows publication 2002-07-19 and stability date 2027 (search excerpts showed other dates **[Contested]**). ISO 80000's "root-power quantity" wording was seen only in search excerpts and is not quoted.
- **Sources:** [NIST SP 811](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication811e2008.pdf); [SI Brochure](https://www.bipm.org/documents/20126/41483022/SI-Brochure-9-EN.pdf); [NIST SP 330](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.330-2019.pdf); [IEC Webstore](https://webstore.iec.ch/en/publication/94). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-130 · Oscilloscope bandwidth and rise time

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0793 and F-0794); documents opened.*

- **Tektronix, *XYZs of Oscilloscopes* primer, 03W-8605-6 (2011)** [Established for the definition; Industry estimate for the rules]: "Oscilloscope bandwidth is specified as the frequency at which a sinusoidal input signal is attenuated to 70.7% of the signal's true amplitude, known as the –3 dB point"; "Oscilloscope Bandwidth ≥ Highest Frequency Component of Signal x5"; "less than ±2% error"; "Bandwidth = k / Rise Time … where k is a value between 0.35 and 0.45 … Oscilloscopes with a bandwidth of <1 GHz typically have a 0.35 value, while oscilloscopes with a bandwidth of > 1 GHz usually have a value between 0.40 and 0.45." (tek.com web pages returned 403; the PDF was opened.)
- **Tektronix, *4 Series B MSO Datasheet*, 48W-74016-7 (22 Apr 2025; distributor-hosted copy)** [Established as Tektronix's specification; **Contested** against the primer's k]: "Bandwidth (calculated rise time) 200 MHz (2.3 ns), 350 MHz (1.3 ns), 500 MHz (900 ps), 1 GHz (450 ps), 1.5 GHz (333 ps)". Implied bandwidth × rise time (our arithmetic): 0.46, 0.455, 0.45, 0.45, 0.4995.
- **Derived (ours):** a single pole gives k = ln 9/(2π) = 0.350 and an amplitude error of 1.94% at one fifth of its bandwidth; a Gaussian response gives 0.340; an ideal brick-wall response gives 0.446.
- **Sources:** [Tektronix primer](https://download.tek.com/document/03W_8605_6.pdf); [4 Series B datasheet (Transcat copy)](https://www.transcat.ca/media/pdf/tektronix-4-series-b-mso-datasheet-new.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-131 · Original filter papers

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file c0708, entries F-0795 to F-0798).*

- **S. Butterworth, "On the Theory of Filter Amplifiers," *Experimental Wireless & The Wireless Engineer*, vol. VII, no. 85, pp. 536–541, October 1930** (Admiralty Research Laboratory; "MS. received by the Editor, May, 1930") [Established; read from a scan of the issue, formulas checked against the page image]: "An ideal electrical filter should not only completely reject the unwanted frequencies but should also have uniform sensitivity for the wanted frequencies"; "the aim is to obtain a filter factor F … of the form F = (1 + x^m)^−½ … where m increases with the number of elements employed"; one LC element tuned to the cutoff with L/C = ½R² gives F = (1 + x⁴)^−½. Sections were separated by valves.
- **R. P. Sallen and E. L. Key, "A Practical Method of Designing RC Active Filters," *IRE Transactions on Circuit Theory*, vol. CT-2, no. 1, pp. 74–85, March 1955, doi:10.1109/TCT.1955.6500159** (MIT Lincoln Laboratory) [Established for the record via Crossref; the paper itself was not opened (ieeexplore.ieee.org is outside the build environment's network)].
- **Sources:** [Crossref record](https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1109/tct.1955.6500159); the Butterworth scan location is recorded in the session facts file. Checked Nov 2026.

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file c0708, entries F-0799 to F-0807); documents opened unless stated.*

- **J. Karki, *Analysis of the Sallen-Key Architecture*, TI SLOA024B (rev. Sep 2002)** [Established]: well above cutoff "Vo/Vi ≈ Zo/R1", rising at 20 dB/dec as the amplifier's open-loop gain falls; "setting K=2.9 results in a nominal Q of 10. Worst case analysis with 1% resistors results in Q=16"; "Use NPO if at all possible. X7R is OK in a pinch. Avoid Z5U"; avoid capacitors below 100 pF.
- **J. Karki, *Active Low-Pass Filter Design*, TI SLOA049D (rev. Feb 2023)** [Established]: "The MFB is generally preferred because the MFB has better sensitivity to component variations and better high-frequency behavior. The unity-gain Sallen-Key inherently has the best gain accuracy"; "Use C0G (NP0) dielectrics"; an output RC of 100 Ω and 47 nF "places a passive pole … at about 40 kHz" (**[Contested]**: the values give 33.9 kHz).
- **T. Kugelstadt, "Active Filter Design Techniques," ch. 16 of *Op Amps For Everyone*, TI SLOD006B (Aug 2002; read from a third-party copy of the TI PDF, TI's links return 404)** [Industry estimate: rules of thumb]: "the open-loop gain (AOL) should be 100 times (40 dB above) the peak gain (Q) of a filter section to allow a maximum gain error of 1%"; "First-order filter: fT = 100·Gain·fc".
- **B. C. Baker, *Designing an anti-aliasing filter for ADCs in the frequency domain*, TI SLYT626 (2Q 2015)** [Industry estimate for the rule; partly **Contested**]: "fGBW = 100 × Q × G × fC"; "An LSB error in dB equals 20 × log [(2N – err)/2N]"; "For a 12-bit ADC where err equals 1 and N equals 12, the gain error equals –2.12 mdB". Its statement that a +38 mdB rise is "fundamental" to a fourth-order Butterworth filter contradicts the ideal (monotonic) Butterworth response.
- **P. Miller and R. Cesari, ch. 13 of *Op Amps For Everyone* (same copy)** [Established; **Contested** within the source on 1 versus ½ LSB]: "Sampling at the Nyquist rate places stringent requirements on the anti-aliasing filter — usually a steep transition 10th or higher order filter is needed"; out-of-band signals to "less than 1 LSB" and elsewhere "≤ ½ LSB".
- **Sources:** [SLOA024B](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/sloa024b/sloa024b.pdf); [SLOA049](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/sloa049b/sloa049b.pdf); [SLYT626](https://www.ti.com/lit/pdf/slyt626); *Op Amps For Everyone* third-party copy (recorded in the facts file). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-133 · Resonator Q by technology

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file c0708, entries F-0808 to F-0819); documents opened unless stated.*

- **J. R. Vig, *Quartz Crystal Resonators and Oscillators … A Tutorial*, U.S. Army CECOM SLCET-TR-88-1 (Rev. 8.5.2.0, Jan 2004)** [Established]: "Q max = 1/(2π fτ)"; "the maximum achievable Q times the frequency is a constant, e.g., 16 million for AT-cut resonators, when f is in MHz"; a quartz frequency standard at "f ~ 5 MHz, Q ~ 10⁶". (A 10⁴–10⁶ range for general-purpose crystals was seen only in an unattributed mirror and is not used.)
- **RFM (Akoustis), RO3101A-20 433.92 MHz SAW resonator datasheet (rev. 06/15/2022)** [Established]: "Unloaded Q QU 9000"; "50 Ω Loaded Q QL 1458"; "Motional Resistance RM 19.4 Ω; Motional Inductance LM 63.8 µH; Motional Capacitance CM 2.11 fF".
- **IEEE International Ultrasonics Symposium 2019 program digests** [Established; research results]: Murata (Nakagawa et al.), I.H.P. SAW "Qmax 3113 3504" with fractional bandwidths 4.0% and 4.1%; Qorvo (Inoue and Solal), layered SAW "very high Q of over 5,000 and high k2 of 8.8% at 1 GHz"; TI (Yen et al.), FBAR, SMR and advanced SAW summarized "With f-Q product 5×10¹² Hz".
- **R. Aigner (TriQuint), "Tunable RF Filters: Pursuing the 'Holy Grail' of Acoustic Filter R&D," *Microwave Journal*, 16 Jun 2008** [Industry estimate; dated]: "Q-values above 2000 at 2GHz represent the state of the art for FBARs and SMR-BAWs".
- **J. N. Burghartz, B. Rejaei, H. Schellevis, "Saddle Add-On Metallization for RF-IC Technology," *IEEE Trans. Electron Devices* 51(3):460–466, Mar 2004, doi:10.1109/TED.2004.823325** [Established]: "A record quality factor of ∼13 for a 10-nH inductor on a conventional 5-Ω-cm silicon substrate is demonstrated" (designed for maximum Q at 1 GHz). No current figure for modern on-chip inductors was opened.
- **Würth Elektronik 744761127A (WE-KI 0603, 27 nH) specification, 2004-10-11 (distributor-hosted)** [Established]: "Q factor 250 MHz Q 40 min."; "Q factor 900 MHz Q 75 typ."; self-resonance "2800 MHz min.".
- **Sources:** [RO3101A-20](https://rfmi.akoustis.com/wp-content/uploads/pdf-front/168796733576212751.pdf); [IUS 2019 Murata](https://ewh.ieee.org/conf/ius/2019/media/files/0107.pdf); [IUS 2019 Qorvo](https://ewh.ieee.org/conf/ius/2019/media/files/0333.pdf); [IUS 2019 TI](https://ewh.ieee.org/conf/ius/2019/media/files/1195.pdf); [Microwave Journal](https://www.microwavejournal.com/articles/12274-tunable-rf-filters-pursuing-the-holy-grail-of-acoustic-filter-r-d); [Würth spec (RS copy)](https://docs.rs-online.com/3c67/0900766b80f88b26.pdf). ## R-135 · Distribution-transformer efficiency standards

*Gathered by a research subagent (Course 07 facts file, entries F-0726 to F-0728); documents opened.*

- **US DOE, "Energy Conservation Program: Energy Conservation Standards for Distribution Transformers," final rule, 89 FR 29834, 22 Apr 2024 (FR Doc. 2024-07480), amending 10 CFR 431.196** [Established]: "Compliance with the amended standards … is required on and after April 23, 2029"; "All efficiency values are at 50 percent of nameplate-rated load" (liquid-immersed) and "at 35 percent of nameplate-rated load" (low-voltage dry-type). Values read from the regulatory tables (2016 level → 2029 level): liquid-immersed single-phase 25 kVA 98.95% → 99.00%, 50 kVA 99.11% → 99.15%, 167 kVA 99.33% → 99.46%; low-voltage dry-type three-phase 75 kVA 98.60% → 98.88%. Single-phase units above 100 kVA (EC1A) and three-phase units of 15 to under 500 kVA (EC2A) were set at a level "expected to predominantly be met via use of amorphous cores"; "DOE estimates that approximately 48,000 metric tons of amorphous will be used … approximately equal to the current stated amorphous capacity (of approximately 45,000 metric tons)". The rule records industry concerns about inrush current and brittleness.
- **US DOE, request for information, 91 FR 35903, 15 Jun 2026 (FR Doc. 2026-11971)** [Established; perishable]: "A Presidential determination issued on April 20, 2026, found that grid infrastructure supply chains, including distribution transformers and electrical core steel, are essential to national defense … DOE is initiating an information and data gathering effort". No later DOE rule changing the standard appeared in the Federal Register through 19 Nov 2026.
- **Sources:** [89 FR 29834 (PDF)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2024-04-22/pdf/2024-07480.pdf); [91 FR 35903](https://www.federalregister.gov/api/v1/documents/2026-11971.json). Checked Nov 2026.
